      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-growth-gluing",
        "exact_quote": "Fix $0<\\tau\\le .001$ among the interface tolerances, before the final choice of $\\delta$, and choose the preceding data with this tolerance. There exist $L_0,t_*,c_0>0$ and $R_1,C<\\infty$ with the following property. Let $f:[t_{\\min},\\infty)\\to(0,1]$ be nonincreasing and locally Lipschitz, equal to one on $[t_{\\min},t_*]$, and a positive multiple of $e^{-\\kappa t/2}$ for sufficiently large $t$. Suppose also that $|f'|\\le C_f^{(0)}f$ for some finite constant. Then for every center and $R\\ge R_1$, writing $s_X=\\mathop{\\mathrm{dist}}(\\cdot,X)$, $$\\label{eq:M3}\n \\begin{aligned}\n &(1-\\tau)\\int_{\\Sigma_g\\cap\\{s_X\\ge4L_0\\}}S_0 f(d)^2\\chi^2+c_0N_R\\\\\n &\\qquad\\le(1+\\tau)\\int_{\\Sigma_g}\\chi^2 f'(d)^2|\\nabla_\\Sigma d|^2\n       +\\frac C{R^2}\\int_{\\Sigma_g}\\chi^2f(d)^2.\n \\end{aligned}$$ In particular $c_0$ is independent of $f$. The constants $L_0,t_*,R_1,C$ can also be chosen independently of $f$; its stated tail and slope conditions are used only to justify exhaustion.",
        "source_id": "PM-375.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-growth-gluing",
          "exact_quote": "Fix $0<\\tau\\le .001$ among the interface tolerances, before the final choice of $\\delta$, and choose the preceding data with this tolerance. Suppose also that $|f'|\\le C_f^{(0)}f$ for some finite constant. Then for every center and $R\\ge R_1$, writing $s_X=\\mathop{\\mathrm{dist}}(\\cdot,X)$, $$\\label{eq:M3}\n \\begin{aligned}\n &(1-\\tau)\\int_{\\Sigma_g\\cap\\{s_X\\ge4L_0\\}}S_0 f(d)^2\\chi^2+c_0N_R\\\\\n &\\qquad\\le(1+\\tau)\\int_{\\Sigma_g}\\chi^2 f'(d)^2|\\nabla_\\Sigma d|^2\n       +\\frac C{R^2}\\int_{\\Sigma_g}\\chi^2f(d)^2.\n \\end{aligned}$$ In particular $c_0$ is independent of $f$.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-growth-multiplier",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\zeta_0=.01$ and $k=1.53$. Increase $t_{\\min}$, before fixing $\\delta$, so that $\\exp(C/(\\gamma t_{\\min}^{\\gamma}))<1.001$, where $C,\\gamma$ dominate the errors in <a href=\"#eq:C8\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:C8\">[eq:C8]</a>. For every finite $t_*\\ge t_{\\min}$, every $\\varepsilon_3>0$, and every $\\eta>0$, there are $t_0>t_*$ and a function $f$ as in Lemma <a href=\"#lem:growth-gluing\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:growth-gluing\">40</a> such that, with $$B_*(t)=1+Ct^{-\\gamma}+\\zeta_0\\mathbf1_{\\{t\\ge t_0\\}},\\quad\n p_*(t)=\\exp\\int_{t_{\\min}}^t\\frac{B_*(s)}s\\,\\,\\mathrm ds,\\quad\n P_*(t)=\\frac1{p_*(t)}\\int_t^\\infty p_*(s)f'(s)^2\\,\\,\\mathrm ds,$$ one has $-P_*'=f'^2+B_*P_*/t$ almost everywhere and $$\\label{eq:M6}\n \\begin{gathered}\n tP_*(t)\\le .79f(t)^2\\qquad(t_{\\min}\\le t<t_0),\\\\\n P_*(t)\\le(\\kappa/4+.001)f(t)^2,\\qquad\n t^3f(t)^2\\le\\varepsilon_3\\qquad(t\\ge t_0),\\\\\n \\sup_{[t_{\\min},t_*]}P_*\\le\\eta.\n \\end{gathered}$$ Moreover $$e^{-\\kappa t}\\le f(t)^2\\le C_f e^{-\\kappa t},\\qquad\n \\Psi(t):=\\int_t^\\infty P_*(s)\\,\\,\\mathrm ds\\le C_f f(t)^2$$ for a finite constant $C_f$ depending on the final construction.",
        "source_id": "PM-375.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-growth-multiplier",
          "exact_quote": "Let $\\zeta_0=.01$ and $k=1.53$. Increase $t_{\\min}$, before fixing $\\delta$, so that $\\exp(C/(\\gamma t_{\\min}^{\\gamma}))<1.001$, where $C,\\gamma$ dominate the errors in <a href=\"#eq:C8\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:C8\">[eq:C8]</a>. For every finite $t_*\\ge t_{\\min}$, every $\\varepsilon_3>0$, and every $\\eta>0$, there are $t_0>t_*$ and a function $f$ as in Lemma <a href=\"#lem:growth-gluing\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:growth-gluing\">40</a> such that, with $$B_*(t)=1+Ct^{-\\gamma}+\\zeta_0\\mathbf1_{\\{t\\ge t_0\\}},\\quad\n p_*(t)=\\exp\\int_{t_{\\min}}^t\\frac{B_*(s)}s\\,\\,\\mathrm ds,\\quad\n P_*(t)=\\frac1{p_*(t)}\\int_t^\\infty p_*(s)f'(s)^2\\,\\,\\mathrm ds,$$ one has $-P_*'=f'^2+B_*P_*/t$ almost everywhere and $$\\label{eq:M6}\n \\begin{gathered}\n tP_*(t)\\le .79f(t)^2\\qquad(t_{\\min}\\le t<t_0),\\\\\n P_*(t)\\le(\\kappa/4+.001)f(t)^2,\\qquad\n t^3f(t)^2\\le\\varepsilon_3\\qquad(t\\ge t_0),\\\\\n \\sup_{[t_{\\min},t_*]}P_*\\le\\eta.\n \\end{gathered}$$ Moreover $$e^{-\\kappa t}\\le f(t)^2\\le C_f e^{-\\kappa t},\\qquad\n \\Psi(t):=\\int_t^\\infty P_*(s)\\,\\,\\mathrm ds\\le C_f f(t)^2$$ for a finite constant $C_f$ depending on the final construction.",
          "source_id": "PM-375.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $\\zeta_0=.01$ and $k=1.53$. Increase $t_{\\min}$, before fixing $\\delta$, so that $\\exp(C/(\\gamma t_{\\min}^{\\gamma}))<1.001$, where $C,\\gamma$ dominate the errors in <a href=\"#eq:C8\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:C8\">[eq:C8]</a>. For every finite $t_*\\ge t_{\\min}$, every $\\varepsilon_3>0$, and every $\\eta>0$, there are $t_0>t_*$ and a function $f$ as in Lemma <a href=\"#lem:growth-gluing\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:growth-gluing\">40</a> such that, with $$B_*(t)=1+Ct^{-\\gamma}+\\zeta_0\\mathbf1_{\\{t\\ge t_0\\}},\\quad\n p_*(t)=\\exp\\int_{t_{\\min}}^t\\frac{B_*(s)}s\\,\\,\\mathrm ds,\\quad\n P_*(t)=\\frac1{p_*(t)}\\int_t^\\infty p_*(s)f'(s)^2\\,\\,\\mathrm ds,$$ one has $-P_*'=f'^2+B_*P_*/t$ almost everywhere and $$\\label{eq:M6}\n \\begin{gathered}\n tP_*(t)\\le .79f(t)^2\\qquad(t_{\\min}\\le t<t_0),\\\\\n P_*(t)\\le(\\kappa/4+.001)f(t)^2,\\qquad\n t^3f(t)^2\\le\\varepsilon_3\\qquad(t\\ge t_0),\\\\\n \\sup_{[t_{\\min},t_*]}P_*\\le\\eta.\n \\end{gathered}$$ Moreover $$e^{-\\kappa t}\\le f(t)^2\\le C_f e^{-\\kappa t},\\qquad\n \\Psi(t):=\\int_t^\\infty P_*(s)\\,\\,\\mathrm ds\\le C_f f(t)^2$$ for a finite constant $C_f$ depending on the final construction.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **stable solution** ([source](#pm-h-3)): A solution $v$ is *stable* if its second variation satisfies $$\label{eq:stability-definition} Q_v(\varphi):= \int_{\mathbb R^n}\bigl(\|\nabla\varphi\|^2+(3v^2-1)\varphi^2\bigr)\,\mathrm dx \ge0 \qquad\text{for every }\varphi\in C_c^1(\mathbb R^n).$$
- **global minimizer** ([source](#pm-h-3)): A *global minimizer* minimizes $\mathcal E$ in every ball among $H^1$ competitors with the same boundary trace.
- **surface tension s_0** ([source](#eq:intro-tension)): $$\label{eq:intro-tension} s_0=\int_{-1}^1\sqrt{2W(t)}\,\mathrm dt =\int_\mathbb Rg'(s)^2\,\mathrm ds =\frac{2\sqrt2}{3}.$$ Under dilations $u(R_jx)$ with $R_j\to\infty$, the minimizing comparison identifies the entire limiting energy measure with $s_0$ times the perimeter measure of a minimizing boundary. This equality needs a recovery sequence patched to the original solution; convergence of the phases alone would leave open the possibility of extra energy.
- **normalized energy density M_r** ([source](#eq:finite-density)): We recall Modica’s energy monotonicity formula (Modica 1989) [[Modica1989](#ref-Modica1989)] and give the stress-tensor calculation in the present normalization. Set $$\label{eq:finite-density} e(v)=\frac12\|\nabla v\|^2+W(v),\qquad M_r(y)=\frac{1}{s_0\omega_m r^m}\int_{B_r(y)}e(v),\qquad M_\infty=\sup_{r>0}M_r(0).$$ The stress tensor $e(v)\mathrm{Id}-\nabla v\otimes\nabla v$ is divergence free. Its contraction with the derivative of $x-y$, followed by the divergence theorem, gives $$r\int_{\partial B_r(y)}\bigl(e(v)-\|\partial_rv\|^2\bigr) =\int_{B_r(y)}\bigl((m+1)e(v)-\|\nabla v\|^2\bigr).$$ Consequently $$\label{eq:finite-monotonicity} M_r'(y)=\frac{1}{s_0\omega_m} \left\{r^{-m-1}\int_{B_r(y)}\left(W(v)-\frac12\|\nabla v\|^2\right) +r^{-m}\int_{\partial B_r(y)}\|\partial_rv\|^2\right\}\ge0.$$ The formula initially holds for almost every $r$ and hence in integrated form for every pair of radii. Containment of shifted balls shows that the limit at infinity is independent of $y$, and $M_r(y)\le M_\infty$.
- **subcritical density** ([source](#pm-h-28)): A number $K_f>0$ is called *subcritical* in dimension $n$ if the conclusion of Theorem <a href="#thm:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:finite">3</a> holds for every bounded entire stable solution with $M_\infty\le K_f$.
- **inverse-profile variables** ([source](#eq:diffuse-variables)): Define the inverse-profile variables $$\label{eq:diffuse-variables} q=1-v^2,\qquad U=g^{-1}(v),\qquad p=\nabla U,\qquad T=\nabla^2U,\qquad D=1-\|p\|^2.$$ Then $0\le D\le1$, $\|p\|\le1$, and direct substitution gives $$\begin{aligned} \Delta U&=-\sqrt2 vD,\label{eq:diffuse-U}\\ \mathcal L D&=2\|T\|^2, &\mathcal L&=-\Delta+2\sqrt2 vp\cdot\nabla+2q\|p\|^2, \label{eq:diffuse-D}\\ (-\Delta+3v^2-1)q&=-q^2D.\label{eq:diffuse-q} \end{aligned}$$ In particular, writing $e(v)=\|\nabla v\|^2/2+W(v)$, $$\label{eq:diffuse-energy} W(v)-\frac12\|\nabla v\|^2=\frac14q^2D, \qquad e(v)=\frac14q^2(2-D).$$ All positive-order derivatives of $U$ have uniform bounds as well. To see this without assuming that $v$ stays uniformly away from its endpoints, on each ball subtract $U$ at its center. The resulting function is uniformly bounded there because $\|p\|\le1$. Equation <a href="#eq:diffuse-U" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:diffuse-U">[eq:diffuse-U]</a> first gives uniform interior $W^{2,r}$ bounds for every finite $r$, then $C^{1,\alpha}$ bounds. Its right side is the smooth function $-\sqrt2 g(U)(1-\|\nabla U\|^2)$; the derivatives of every translate of $g$ are uniformly bounded. Interior Schauder estimates and induction give the assertion.
- **normal average I_P** ([source](#eq:diffuse-normal-average)): The limit inequality now gives a uniform estimate for the original solution. For a regular zero $z$ and a finite $P>0$, write $$\label{eq:diffuse-normal-average} I_P(z)=\frac1\sigma\int_{-P}^P q(z+t\nu(z))^3D(z+t\nu(z))\,\,\mathrm dt.$$ This is a normal average, not the pointwise defect. Its normalization is chosen so that the limiting mass is $\sigma^{-1}\int G^2F$. The next lemma proves $\|H\|\le cI_P$ by first fixing the interval and only then choosing the defect threshold.
- **penalized reach d** ([source](#eq:reach-definition)): $$\label{eq:reach-definition} d(z)=\inf_{y\in\Sigma\setminus\{z\}}\ell\Gamma(s),\qquad \ell=\|y-z\|,\quad e=\frac{y-z}{\ell},\quad s=\|\nu(z)\cdot e\|.$$ A competitor with $s=0$ has value $+\infty$. An *active contact* is a competitor attaining this infimum, or a limiting diagonal competitor attaining it as the second point approaches the base. The proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:reach-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:reach-calculus">26</a> will show that one of these alternatives always realizes the infimum.

|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-surface-tension | surface tension s_0 | = | $\frac{2\sqrt2}{3}$ | none |
| q-normal-average-constant | normal-average constant sigma | = | $\frac{4\sqrt2}{3}$ | none |
| q-unit-density-limit | limiting normalized density of the monotone solution | = | $1$ | none |
| q-energy-growth | energy in a ball of radius R | ≤ | $C_N R^{N-1}$ | R at least two |
| q-reach-curvature | reach times maximal principal curvature | ≤ | $2$ | all regular points |
| q-mean-curvature-coefficient | mean curvature relative to normal average | ≤ | $\frac1{2\sqrt2}+\varepsilon$ | epsilon positive, P large |
| q-sharp-interaction | normal average at adjacent gap | ≥ | $(32-\varepsilon)e^{-\sqrt2\ell_{\min}(z)}$ | epsilon positive, N large |
| q-phase-tail | amplitude between adjacent sheets | ≤ | $(4+\varepsilon)$ | epsilon positive, N large |
| q-fourth-order-growth | weighted fourth-order mass | ≤ | $C K(R)R^{m-4}$ | R large |
| q-radial-reach-budget | radial reach budget | ≤ | $.73\mathcal L$ | R and B large |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-end-profiles** [claim](#stmt-lem-monotone-ends) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-monotone-ends): Ground-state identity from the positive derivative, translation compactness, long-cylinder test, calibration. -> stable seven-dimensional end profiles that minimize
- **step-minimizing-barriers** [claim](#stmt-lem-monotone-minimizer) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-monotone-minimizer): Clip a minimizer between end profiles and slide translates with strong comparison. -> global minimality and energy growth
- **step-exact-perimeter-limit** [claim](#stmt-prop-monotone-exact-limit) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-monotone-exact-limit): BV compactness, recovery sequence patched in a thin layer, averaging over layers. -> energy measure converges to perimeter measure
- **step-flat-directed-cone** [claim](#stmt-lem-monotone-flat-cone) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-monotone-flat-cone): Singular set at most the vertex, Jacobi equation on the link, total geodesy and translation invariance cases. -> hyperplane of multiplicity one
- **step-monotone-rigidity** [claim](#stmt-thm-monotone) / [proof](#pm-h-15): Unit density from the flat cone, near-unit-density theorem, ODE integration for the profile. -> planar profile
- **step-uniform-defect-estimates** [claim](#stmt-lem-diffuse-local) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-diffuse-local): Weak Harnack, mean-value inequality, exponential barrier, normalized Schauder estimates. -> uniform defect estimates
- **step-phase-clearing** [claim](#stmt-lem-diffuse-phase) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-diffuse-phase): Eigenfunction subsolution comparison and exponential radial supersolution. -> zero set and decay
- **step-small-defect-limits** [claim](#stmt-lem-diffuse-compactness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-diffuse-compactness): Compactness from normalized bounds and conjugation of the defect equation. -> limit operator and mean curvature limit
- **step-positive-decomposition** [claim](#stmt-lem-diffuse-positive) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-diffuse-positive): Monotone exhaustion, extreme point classification, convexity of the mass ratio, Krein-Milman. -> sharp normal derivative bound
- **step-mean-curvature-defect** [claim](#stmt-lem-diffuse-mean) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-diffuse-mean): Contradiction sequence and passage to the normalized limit. -> mean curvature bound
- **step-critical-solution** [claim](#stmt-lem-finite-critical) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-finite-critical): Literature reduction to a critical density, density transfer and sheet counting. -> critical solution with ordered sheets
- **step-tube-mass-hardy** [claim](#stmt-lem-finite-measures) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-finite-measures): Tube injectivity, flux tail, smooth cutoff, Hardy test. -> mass and Hardy estimates
- **step-nearest-gap** [claim](#stmt-lem-finite-gap) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-finite-gap): Distance Hessian to adjacent sheet, Jacobian and multiplicity count. -> gap Laplacian bound
- **step-green-inequality** [claim](#stmt-lem-finite-green) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-finite-green): Test the concave gap inequality against a radial Green kernel. -> integral gap inequality
- **step-rough-gap** [claim](#stmt-lem-finite-rough) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-finite-rough): Average estimate plus Harnack for adjacent height differences. -> rough gap bound
- **step-finite-density-rigidity** [claim](#stmt-thm-finite) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-finite-rough-2): Nearly linear gap test, upper coefficient from mean curvature, lower coefficient from endpoint defect, incompatibility. -> no critical solution
- **step-uniform-regularity** [claim](#stmt-cor-finite-subcritical) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-finite-subcritical): Every finite threshold is subcritical, so cited sheeting results apply. -> uniform local regularity
- **step-elementary-chord** [claim](#stmt-lem-reach-small-defect) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-reach-small-defect): Chord quotients, compactness of small-defect solutions. -> elementary reach bounds
- **step-density-window** [claim](#stmt-lem-reach-density-window) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-reach-density-window): Slab confinement and delayed monotonicity iteration. -> bounded density at near reach scale
- **step-half-line-barrier** [claim](#stmt-lem-reach-half-line-barrier) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-reach-half-line-barrier): Reduction of order for the linearized operator. -> positive bounded supersolution
- **step-isolated-graph** [claim](#stmt-lem-reach-isolation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-reach-isolation): Subcritical regularity then two parity projections using the spectral gap. -> single graph with improved mean curvature derivatives
- **step-phase-ball** [claim](#stmt-lem-reach-phase-ball) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-reach-phase-ball): Subsolution comparison along the inward radius and Poisson kernel bounds. -> one-sided trace improvement
- **step-reach-inequality** [claim](#stmt-prop-reach-calculus) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-reach-calculus): Semiconcavity, touching branches per contact type, inverse Jacobian transfer. -> reach differential inequality
- **step-reach-base-defect** [claim](#stmt-cor-reach-base-defect) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-reach-base-defect): Tangent balls and exponential comparison. -> reach bounds near bad zeros
- **step-sharp-means** [claim](#stmt-lem-interface-sharp-means) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-interface-sharp-means): Normalized limit, Green kernel comparison, contradiction sequence. -> sharp means
- **step-mean-derivative** [claim](#stmt-lem-interface-mean-gradient) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-interface-mean-gradient): Extreme point profiles and averaging on a surface disk. -> averaged mean derivative bound
- **step-lifted-test** [claim](#stmt-lem-interface-immersed-test) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-interface-immersed-test): Square root of summed squares of local inverse branches. -> admissible pushed-down test
- **step-strengthened-stability** [claim](#stmt-prop-interface-strengthened-stability) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-interface-strengthened-stability): Expand the lifted form and gain from the potential deficit. -> surface stability inequality
- **step-halfspace-inverse** [claim](#stmt-lem-interface-halfspace-inverse) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-interface-halfspace-inverse): Ground-state substitution and exponential barriers. -> weighted inverse
- **step-exact-comparison** [claim](#stmt-prop-interface-exact-comparison) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-interface-halfspace-inverse-2): Opposite sheet kernel bound; artificial half-space solutions with cancellation; shifted barrier. -> defect-interaction comparison
- **step-good-area** [claim](#stmt-lem-interface-good-area) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-interface-good-area): Graph charts and packing. -> area bound
- **step-weighted-integral** [claim](#stmt-lem-interface-integral) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-interface-integral): Young inequality and convolution replacing the supremum. -> weighted integral comparison
- **step-clean-windows** [claim](#stmt-lem-interface-log-windows) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-interface-log-windows): Graph continuation, angle estimates, unique projection. -> clean window geometry
- **step-sharp-gaps** [claim](#stmt-prop-interface-sharp-gaps) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-interface-sharp-gaps): Kernel integration and a barrier built from principal tails. -> sharp coefficients
- **step-curvature-moment** [claim](#stmt-lem-growth-curvature) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-growth-curvature): Simons identity, strict Kato gain, stability. -> curvature moment bound
- **step-uniform-gluing** [claim](#stmt-lem-growth-gluing) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-growth-gluing): Square-root sum of ambient and lifted amplitudes with variance cancellation. -> glued stability inequality
- **step-reach-multiplier** [claim](#stmt-lem-growth-multiplier) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-growth-multiplier): Piecewise weight with plateau, slow decay, power decay and exponential tail. -> multiplier bounds
- **step-fourth-order-growth** [claim](#stmt-prop-growth-fourth-order) / [proof](#pm-h-74): Integrate the reach inequality against the multiplier and absorb into the gluing and curvature estimates. -> fourth-order growth bound
- **step-weight-identities** [claim](#stmt-lem-radial-weight) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-radial-weight): Change of variables and chain rule with semiconcave reach. -> weight identities
- **step-summable-errors** [claim](#stmt-lem-radial-errors) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-radial-errors): Net counting from the fourth-order bound and dyadic summation. -> negligible errors
- **step-radial-reach-budget** [claim](#stmt-lem-radial-reach-budget) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-radial-reach-budget): Integrate the reach inequality against the radial test and transfer feet. -> reach budget
- **step-radial-geometry** [claim](#stmt-lem-radial-clean-geometry) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-radial-clean-geometry): Apply the clean window lemma away from the rough set. -> band geometry
- **step-switching-cost** [claim](#stmt-lem-radial-switch-cost) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-radial-switch-cost): Exact one-dimensional interpolation integral and a supremum computation for the loss. -> stability inequality with small switching cost
- **step-small-reach-area** [claim](#stmt-lem-radial-area) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-radial-area): Transfer energy to surface area and use jointly injective tubes to exclude large reach. -> area lower bound
- **step-stable-rigidity** [claim](#stmt-thm-stable) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-radial-area-2): Coefficient comparison of reach and stability inequalities contradicts the area bound. -> stable classification

        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-radial-weight",
          "exact_quote": "Uniformly for $\\tau\\ge\\log R$ and $t\\ge t_{\\min}$, where $t_{\\min}>0$ is any fixed lower bound for the reach on the good set, $$\\label{eq:R1}\n\\begin{split}\n \\mathcal F_\\tau\n &=(1+o_R(1))\\,cW_*\n       \\left(\\frac{t}{c\\tau\\vee b(t)}\\right)^\\vartheta,\\\\\n \\frac{\\mathcal F_\\tau+\\mathcal F}{W_*}&\\le C(L+\\tau),\\qquad\n \\frac{\\mathcal F}{W_*}\\ge\\frac1{q_*+\\vartheta/t_{\\min}},\\\\\n \\mathcal F&\\le C(L+\\tau)^{1-\\vartheta}.\n\\end{split}$$ On the regular zero surface the following is a distributional measure inequality: $$\\label{eq:R2}\n\\begin{split}\n \\Delta_\\Sigma\\mathcal F\\ge{}&\n \\mathcal F_\\tau\\Delta_\\Sigma\\tau\n -c^2\\vartheta J_1|\\nabla_\\Sigma\\tau|^2\n -W_*(\\Delta_\\Sigma d)_{\\rm upper}\\\\\n &+\\frac{\\vartheta W_*}{d}|\\nabla_\\Sigma d|^2\n +\\frac{W_*\\lambda}{b'}|\\nabla_\\Sigma(c\\tau-b(d))|^2,\\\\\n J_1={}&\\int_{b(d)}^\\infty\n u^{-\\vartheta-1}f_*'(c\\tau-u)\\,\\mathrm du\n \\le C\\mathcal F_\\tau/\\tau.\n\\end{split}$$ The last square is defined to be zero where $b'=0$.",

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-stable-rigidity-dim7**: Every entire stable bounded solution of the quartic Allen-Cahn equation in seven variables is one of the two constant wells or a planar one-dimensional transition; no energy-growth hypothesis is used.
- **inv-monotone-rigidity-dim8**: An entire solution in eight variables with values in (-1,1) and strictly positive derivative in the last coordinate is a planar transition with a unit direction having positive last component.
- **inv-finite-density-rigidity**: For ambient dimension between four and seven, a stable solution whose normalized energy density supremum is finite is a constant well or a planar transition.
- **inv-modica-bound**: Every bounded entire solution of the equation has kinetic energy density at most the potential at each point.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-monotone-dim-nine**: Applying the monotone rigidity conclusion to bounded strictly monotone solutions in nine or more variables. -> Nonplanar monotone solutions exist there; the argument relies on the absence of a singular ray in the limiting cone, which fails.
- **anti-stable-dim-eight**: Concluding planarity of stable solutions in eight variables from the seven-dimensional classification. -> Bounded stable nonplanar solutions exist in dimension eight, so the conclusion is false.
- **anti-stability-as-minimality**: Treating a stable solution as an energy minimizer on every ball. -> Stability only gives a quadratic inequality, so minimizing comparisons and perimeter-limit arguments are not justified.
- **anti-finite-window-sharp-constant**: Using the sharp endpoint coefficient with a finite normal window. -> Finite truncation strictly decreases the denominator, so the endpoint constant cannot be retained.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The main results are rigidity and classification theorems for entire solutions of a semilinear elliptic equation, proved by compactness, contradiction and comparison arguments; they involve infinite-dimensional objects and no finite procedure computes a bound or object from finite input. The only explicit numerical constants (surface tension and normal-average normalization) are fixed closed-form values, not outputs of an algorithm.

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The theorems classify entire stable or monotone solutions of the Allen–Cahn equation on whole Euclidean space. They give no finite procedure, so there is no routine to implement. A library could at most use the planar profile as a test fixture. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The end-profile lemma concerns limits of an entire solution along a monotone direction in seven dimensions. It is a purely analytic statement with no time evolution or simulation step. It gives no scheme for building phase-field effects. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The finite-density theorem is an existence-type rigidity statement over a dimension range. It does not describe an operation a tool could carry out on data. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The reach-calculus proposition is a set of differential inequalities for a distance-like function on a zero surface. It is used inside a contradiction argument and is not a computational procedure with inputs and outputs. Its proof relies on measurable selection and compactness, not on a finite construction. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The nearest-sheet distance lemma controls a geometric function on a smooth surface with multiplicity bounds. It defines no storable structure, index or query operation. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The area lower bound is a statement about zero sets of exact solutions used for a contradiction. It has no numeric kernel, precision requirement or dataflow that hardware could exploit. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The existence of a nonempty zero set for a bounded solution has no relation to hardness assumptions, adversaries or secrecy. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The mean-curvature bound concerns a scalar semilinear equation with a fixed quartic potential. It supplies no learning guarantee, loss landscape result or generalisation statement for a trained model. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The exact-limit proposition relates blown-down energies of global minimizers to perimeter. It is a qualitative convergence result and gives no optimisation algorithm, convergence rate or bound on an objective that a solver could use. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The isolation lemma describes local graph structure of one zero sheet at a large scale. It contains no notion of nodes, messages, failures or consensus. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The flat-cone lemma has value only inside geometric measure theory and PDE, as a step reducing the eight-dimensional monotone theorem to a hyperplane. It has no practical construction outside that research context. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-monotone-dimension-eight** (counterexample_scope): Monotone rigidity is proved only in ambient dimension eight. In dimension nine the singular set of a minimizing cone may be a whole axis, and the known nonplanar strictly monotone solutions realise exactly this possibility. Blocked use: ['Concluding that a bounded strictly monotone entire solution in nine or more variables is planar', 'Using the eight-dimensional cone-flatness lemma as a substitute for the missing exclusion of a singular ray in higher dimension']
- **limit-stable-dimension-seven** (counterexample_scope): The stable classification stops at dimension seven: bounded stable nonplanar solutions exist in dimension eight, so the conclusion fails there. Blocked use: ['Deducing planarity of an eight-dimensional stable solution from the seven-dimensional theorem', 'Applying the reach and radial-comparison argument with interface dimension other than six']
- **limit-stability-not-minimality** (hypothesis): Stability is only the nonnegativity of the second variation, which is weaker than minimization. Perimeter-limit and comparison arguments need minimality, which the paper obtains separately in the monotone setting through the end profiles. Blocked use: ['Treating a stable solution as an energy minimizer in every ball', 'Applying the exact perimeter-limit proposition to a merely stable solution']
- **limit-finite-window-endpoint** (approximation): The sharp endpoint coefficient of the mean-curvature bound cannot be kept with a finite normal window; a strictly larger coefficient is required, and the strictness is used in the final contradiction. Blocked use: ['Using the endpoint coefficient with a finite normal interval', 'Replacing the strict inequality on the coefficient by a non-strict one in the finite-density argument']
- **limit-finite-density-hypothesis** (hypothesis): Theorem 3 is conditional on a finite supremum of the normalized density. Removing that condition is done in the paper only for stable solutions in seven variables, through the unbounded-density argument with interface dimension six. Blocked use: ['Using the finite-density theorem without verifying that the density supremum is finite', 'Assuming that rigidity of stable solutions without a density bound is established in dimensions four to six']
- **limit-entire-solutions-only** (formal_scope): All rigidity statements concern solutions defined on the whole Euclidean space with values in the closed or open interval between the wells; boundary data or bounded domains are outside the scope. Blocked use: ['Applying the classification to solutions on a bounded domain with boundary conditions', 'Applying it to vector-valued or non-quartic nonlinearities']
- **limit-nonconstructive-thresholds** (nonconstructive): Several thresholds and constants come from contradiction sequences or depend on a fixed critical solution, so they are not computed; the proofs give no algorithm and no explicit values. Blocked use: ['Quoting explicit numerical thresholds for the defect parameter in the mean-curvature bound', 'Using the constants of the critical-solution lemma uniformly over all solutions']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We resolve De Giorgi's conjecture positively in dimension eight: every entire <i>C</i><sup>2</sup> solution $`u:\mathbb R^8\to(-1,1)`$ of $`\Delta u=u^3-u`$ with an everywhere positive directional derivative is a planar heteroclinic. We prove that every stable solution $`v:\mathbb R^7\to[-1,1]`$ of this equation is a constant well or a planar heteroclinic, without an energy-growth assumption.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>

<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
##### Introduction

<a id="eq:allen-cahn"></a>
Consider the scalar Allen–Cahn equation $$\label{eq:allen-cahn}
 \Delta u=u^3-u.$$ The function $$g(s)=\tanh(s/\sqrt2)$$ is its increasing one-dimensional transition from $-1$ to $1$. Thus $u(x)=g(e\cdot x-c)$ solves <a href="#eq:allen-cahn" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:allen-cahn">[eq:allen-cahn]</a> whenever $e$ is a unit vector; its level sets are parallel hyperplanes. De Giorgi’s conjecture asks whether every bounded entire solution that is strictly increasing in a fixed direction has this form in ambient dimension at most eight (De Giorgi 1979) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[DeGiorgi1979](#ref-DeGiorgi1979)].

The conjecture connects phase transitions with the Bernstein problem for minimal hypersurfaces. For the quartic potential $$W(s)=\frac14(1-s^2)^2,$$ the rescaled phase-transition energies have surface tension times perimeter as their variational limit (Modica and Mortola 1977; Modica 1987) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[ModicaMortola1977](#ref-ModicaMortola1977), [Modica1987](#ref-Modica1987)]. The graph condition in the minimal-surface problem is important: nonflat minimizing cones occur in ambient dimension eight, whereas nonaffine entire minimal graphs first occur in ambient dimension nine (Simons 1968; Bombieri, De Giorgi, and Giusti 1969) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Simons1968](#ref-Simons1968), [BDG1969](#ref-BDG1969)]. The corresponding distinction between stable and monotone Allen–Cahn solutions will also enter our proof.

Theorem <a href="#thm:stable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:stable">2</a> has a geometric parallel in the stable Bernstein theorem for complete, connected, two-sided stable smooth minimal immersions of boundaryless six-manifolds into $\mathbb R^7$: their images are entire affine hyperplanes, with no volume-growth hypothesis (Hong, Li, and Wang 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[HongLiWang2026](#ref-HongLiWang2026)]. The Bernstein theorem is not an input to our argument.

Ghoussoub and Gui established the conjecture in dimension two (Ghoussoub and Gui 1998) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[GhoussoubGui1998](#ref-GhoussoubGui1998)], and Ambrosio and Cabré in dimension three (Ambrosio and Cabré 2000) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[AmbrosioCabre2000](#ref-AmbrosioCabre2000)]. Savin proved the result through dimension eight under the additional assumption $$\lim_{t\to-\infty}u(x',t)=-1,\qquad
 \lim_{t\to+\infty}u(x',t)=1
 \quad\text{for every }x'\in\mathbb R^{n-1}$$ (Savin 2009, Theorem 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Savin2009](#ref-Savin2009)]. These limits are pointwise in the transverse variable. Del Pino, Kowalczyk and Wei constructed nonplanar strictly monotone solutions in every ambient dimension at least nine, near large dilations of nonaffine minimal graphs (Pino, Kowalczyk, and Wei 2011) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[DPKW2011](#ref-DPKW2011)]. The main result of this paper gives a positive resolution in dimension eight without an assumption on the directional limits.

<a id="stmt-thm-monotone"></a>
<div id="thm:monotone" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (Monotone rigidity in dimension eight). *Let $u\in C^2(\mathbb R^8,(-1,1))$ satisfy $$\Delta u=u^3-u,\qquad \partial_8u>0\quad\text{in }\mathbb R^8.$$ Then there are $e\in\mathbb S^7$ with $e_8>0$ and $c\in\mathbb R$ such that $$u(x)=\tanh\!\left(\frac{e\cdot x-c}{\sqrt2}\right)
 \qquad\text{for every }x\in\mathbb R^8.$$*

</div>

Neither energy growth nor minimization is assumed. Those properties, and the well limits, will be consequences of the argument. Both rigidity theorems concern the scalar quartic equation <a href="#eq:allen-cahn" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:allen-cahn">[eq:allen-cahn]</a>; the monotonicity assumption in Theorem <a href="#thm:monotone" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:monotone">1</a> is strict positivity of $\partial_8u$.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
###### The stable equation

For an open set $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ write $$\mathcal E(v;\Omega)
   =\int_\Omega\left(\frac12|\nabla v|^2+W(v)\right)\,\mathrm dx.$$ A solution $v$ is *stable* if its second variation satisfies <a id="eq:stability-definition"></a>$$\label{eq:stability-definition}
 Q_v(\varphi):=
 \int_{\mathbb R^n}\bigl(|\nabla\varphi|^2+(3v^2-1)\varphi^2\bigr)\,\mathrm dx
 \ge0
 \qquad\text{for every }\varphi\in C_c^1(\mathbb R^n).$$ A *global minimizer* minimizes $\mathcal E$ in every ball among $H^1$ competitors with the same boundary trace. Stability requires only the quadratic inequality, so it is a weaker condition than minimization.

A strictly positive directional derivative solves the linearized equation and gives stability. Moreover, the limits along that direction are stable solutions in one fewer variable. This observation makes the following classification the analytic core of the paper.

<a id="stmt-thm-stable"></a>
<div id="thm:stable" class="theorem">

**Theorem 2** (Stable rigidity in dimension seven). *Let $v\in C^2(\mathbb R^7,[-1,1])$ be a stable solution of $\Delta v=v^3-v$. Then either $v\equiv1$, $v\equiv-1$, or $$v(x)=\tanh\!\left(\frac{a\cdot x-b}{\sqrt2}\right)
 \qquad\text{for some }a\in\mathbb S^6,\ b\in\mathbb R.$$ No energy-growth assumption is required.*

</div>

This stability endpoint is sharp: Pacard and Wei constructed bounded stable nonplanar solutions in dimension eight (Pacard and Wei 2013) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[PacardWei2013](#ref-PacardWei2013)]. For the smooth quartic equation, Liu, Luo, Wang, Juncheng Wei, Yong Wei and Wu prove stable rigidity in dimension three and monotone rigidity in dimension four (Liu et al. 2026) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[LLWWW2026](#ref-LLWWW2026)]. The same conclusions are obtained independently by Chan, Fernández-Real, Figalli, Florit-Simon and Serra (Chan, Fernández-Real, Figalli, Florit-Simon, et al. 2026, Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[CFFFS2026](#ref-CFFFS2026)]. Florit-Simon and Serra prove stable rigidity in dimension four under a bounded-energy-density hypothesis (Florit-Simon and Serra 2025) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[FSS2025](#ref-FSS2025)]. Both the higher dimension and the removal of the density assumption matter here; they require different parts of the analysis.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
###### From stable end profiles to the monotone theorem

We give the complete deduction of Theorem <a href="#thm:monotone" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:monotone">1</a> from Theorem <a href="#thm:stable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:stable">2</a> at the beginning of the paper. The comparison with competitors between the directional limits is due to Alberti, Ambrosio and Cabré (Alberti, Ambrosio, and Cabré 2001, Theorem 4.4) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[AlbertiAmbrosioCabre2001](#ref-AlbertiAmbrosioCabre2001)]. Jerison and Monneau established the minimizing-end-profile reduction (Jerison and Monneau 2004, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[JerisonMonneau2004](#ref-JerisonMonneau2004)]. The positive derivative supplies stability, and the directional limits are stable seven-dimensional solutions. Their classification allows constant wells or planar transitions at this stage. Each is a global minimizer, by the one-dimensional calibration. Clipping a competitor between the two end profiles and sliding translates of $u$ then shows that $u$ is itself a global minimizer. Comparison with a constant phase inside a large ball gives $\mathcal E(u;B_R)\le CR^7$.

The surface tension in this normalization is <a id="eq:intro-tension"></a>$$\label{eq:intro-tension}
 s_0=\int_{-1}^1\sqrt{2W(t)}\,\mathrm dt
     =\int_\mathbb Rg'(s)^2\,\mathrm ds
     =\frac{2\sqrt2}{3}.$$ Under dilations $u(R_jx)$ with $R_j\to\infty$, the minimizing comparison identifies the entire limiting energy measure with $s_0$ times the perimeter measure of a minimizing boundary. This equality needs a recovery sequence patched to the original solution; convergence of the phases alone would leave open the possibility of extra energy.

Energy monotonicity makes the limiting boundary a cone, that is, a boundary preserved by dilations about the origin. Directional monotonicity fixes the sign of one component of its normal. In dimension eight the minimizing-boundary regularity theory allows only a possible singularity at the vertex (Federer 1970) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Federer1970](#ref-Federer1970)]. The classical directed-cone argument then forces a hyperplane (Schnürer 2003) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Schnuerer2003](#ref-Schnuerer2003)]. We include the case in which that normal component vanishes identically: it gives translation invariance, which is incompatible with an isolated singular vertex. The limiting density is therefore that of one planar transition. Wang’s near-unit-density theorem (Wang 2017, Theorem 11.1) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Wang2017](#ref-Wang2017)] returns to the original solution, and the one-dimensional equation determines the profile and its orientation.

This deduction explains why the stable theorem is the right target. It also separates the classical geometric reduction from the new estimates needed for stable solutions. A reader interested primarily in the conjecture can follow the deduction first; a reader interested in the stable classification can proceed directly from its statement to the analytic argument in Part II. The classical gradient bound and energy monotonicity needed by both paths are proved in Section <a href="#sec:classical-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:classical-estimates">2</a>.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### The finite-density step

Let $\omega_{n-1}$ be the volume of the unit ball in $\mathbb R^{n-1}$. For a solution in $\mathbb R^n$ define $$M_R(y)=\frac{\mathcal E(v;B_R(y))}
              {s_0\omega_{n-1}R^{n-1}},\qquad
 M_\infty=\sup_{R>0}M_R(0).$$ A single planar transition has limiting density one. The first analytic step is the following result, which is also needed locally in the argument that removes the density bound.

<a id="stmt-thm-finite"></a>
<div id="thm:finite" class="theorem">

**Theorem 3** (Finite-density stable rigidity). *Let $4\le n\le7$. Suppose $v\in C^2(\mathbb R^n,[-1,1])$ solves $\Delta v=v^3-v$ and satisfies $$\int_{\mathbb R^n}\bigl(|\nabla\varphi|^2+(3v^2-1)\varphi^2\bigr)\,\mathrm dx
 \ge0
 \qquad\text{for every }\varphi\in C_c^1(\mathbb R^n).$$ If $M_\infty<\infty$, then $v\equiv1$, $v\equiv-1$, or $v(x)=g(a\cdot x-b)$ for some $a\in\mathbb S^{n-1}$ and $b\in\mathbb R$.*

</div>

For stable solutions in $\mathbb R^7$, the remaining task is to prove that $M_\infty$ is finite. A penalized reach measures both the bending of a zero sheet and its proximity to other sheets; the finite-density theorem supplies local regularity at nearly this scale. Combining reach geometry with stability bounds the fourth curvature moment and squared defect, controlling the regions where the zero set is poorly approximated by planes. We then compare the reach inequality with an ambient stability test built from the same radial amplitude on the sheets. The cost of joining neighboring amplitudes is small enough to leave a strict positive margin, which contradicts the amount of surface area forced by unbounded density.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### Organization and conventions

Part I gives the complete deduction from stable end profiles to Theorem <a href="#thm:monotone" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:monotone">1</a>, conditional on Theorem <a href="#thm:stable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:stable">2</a>. Appendix <a href="#sec:dimension-nine" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:dimension-nine">2</a> explains compatibility with the dimension-nine construction of del Pino, Kowalczyk and Wei. After an optional guide to the analytical strategy in Section <a href="#sec:analytic-strategy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:analytic-strategy">1</a>, Part II proves the diffuse estimates, Theorem <a href="#thm:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:finite">3</a>, and its local regularity consequence. Part III develops the reach and interface estimates, joins the ambient and surface tests, and proves fourth-moment growth. Part IV constructs the radial ambient test and completes the unbounded-density contradiction. Thus the reading order places the geometric application first, while the analytic proof of the stable theorem is contained in Parts II–IV. For a direct analytical reading, begin at Section <a href="#sec:diffuse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:diffuse">2</a>, whose opening recalls the equation, stability and all normalizations used there; the strategy section can be read beforehand or consulted later.

We write $n=m+1$, so $m$ is the interface dimension, and use $\,\mathrm dS$ for induced surface measure. The surface tension $s_0$ and the normal-average constant $\sigma=2s_0$ are kept distinct. All stability tests are unrestricted compactly supported tests. Uniformity in translating centers, the order of parameter choices, and successive limits are specified in the statements where they are needed.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
##### Two classical estimates for the quartic equation

Both the geometric deduction and the analytical classification use the same gradient bound and energy monotonicity. We record their short proofs here, independently of either rigidity theorem. Let $n=m+1\ge2$ and let $v\in C^2(\mathbb R^n,[-1,1])$ solve $\Delta v=v^3-v$. Throughout, $$W(t)=\frac14(1-t^2)^2,\qquad s_0=\frac{2\sqrt2}{3},$$ and $\omega_m$ denotes the volume of the unit ball in $\mathbb R^m$. Interior elliptic estimates bootstrap $v$ to a smooth function with uniform bounds for every derivative of fixed order.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
###### Modica’s gradient estimate

<a id="eq:diffuse-modica"></a>
We use Modica’s gradient bound (Modica 1985) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Modica1985](#ref-Modica1985)], $$\label{eq:diffuse-modica}
 \frac12|\nabla v|^2\le W(v).$$ For completeness, its entire-solution argument in this normalization is short. For $P=|\nabla v|^2/2-W(v)$, the identities $\nabla P=\nabla^2v\nabla v-W'(v)\nabla v$ and $\Delta P=|\nabla^2v|^2-W'(v)^2$ imply, wherever $\nabla v\ne0$, $$\Delta P\ge
 \frac{2W'(v)}{|\nabla v|^2}\nabla v\cdot\nabla P.$$ If $\sup P>0$, translate a maximizing sequence and pass to a locally smooth limit. Its $P$ attains a positive maximum. The strong maximum principle makes $P$ constant on the component of $\{\nabla v\ne0\}$ containing that point. This component is also closed: on it $|\nabla v|^2\ge2\sup P>0$. It is therefore all of $\mathbb R^n$. The globally bounded smooth vector field $\nabla v$ has complete forward flow, and along that flow $v$ increases at rate at least $2\sup P$, contradicting its boundedness. This proves <a href="#eq:diffuse-modica" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:diffuse-modica">[eq:diffuse-modica]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
###### Normalized energy and monotonicity

<a id="eq:finite-density"></a>
<a id="eq:finite-monotonicity"></a>
We recall Modica’s energy monotonicity formula (Modica 1989) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Modica1989](#ref-Modica1989)] and give the stress-tensor calculation in the present normalization. Set $$\label{eq:finite-density}
 e(v)=\frac12|\nabla v|^2+W(v),\qquad
 M_r(y)=\frac{1}{s_0\omega_m r^m}\int_{B_r(y)}e(v),\qquad
 M_\infty=\sup_{r>0}M_r(0).$$ The stress tensor $e(v)\mathrm{Id}-\nabla v\otimes\nabla v$ is divergence free. Its contraction with the derivative of $x-y$, followed by the divergence theorem, gives $$r\int_{\partial B_r(y)}\bigl(e(v)-|\partial_rv|^2\bigr)
   =\int_{B_r(y)}\bigl((m+1)e(v)-|\nabla v|^2\bigr).$$ Consequently $$\label{eq:finite-monotonicity}
 M_r'(y)=\frac{1}{s_0\omega_m}
 \left\{r^{-m-1}\int_{B_r(y)}\left(W(v)-\frac12|\nabla v|^2\right)
       +r^{-m}\int_{\partial B_r(y)}|\partial_rv|^2\right\}\ge0.$$ The formula initially holds for almost every $r$ and hence in integrated form for every pair of radii. Containment of shifted balls shows that the limit at infinity is independent of $y$, and $M_r(y)\le M_\infty$.

The derivation uses neither stability nor a density-growth hypothesis.

<a id="sec:analytic-strategy"></a>
<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
<a id="sec:radial"></a>
<a id="sec:reach"></a>
### The geometric deduction

<a id="sec:monotone"></a>
##### From stable end profiles to the monotone theorem

In this part we deduce Theorem <a href="#thm:monotone" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:monotone">1</a> from the unrestricted seven-dimensional classification in Theorem <a href="#thm:stable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:stable">2</a>, whose independent analytical proof occupies Parts II–IV. None of those parts uses the geometric deduction given here. The end profiles will initially be allowed to be planar heteroclinics. Their minimizing property suffices for the argument; no well limit is imposed on the given solution. We first derive minimization and surface-order energy growth, then identify the complete limiting energy with perimeter. Only after this exact identification do we use the geometry of a directed minimizing cone and return to the equation at unit density.

We use the energy $\mathcal E$ and global minimizers defined in Section <a href="#sec:introduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:introduction">1</a>. It is enough to consider competitors valued in $[-1,1]$: truncation to this interval decreases both terms of the energy. This definition also gives minimization in every bounded Lipschitz domain: extend a same-trace competitor by $u$ to a containing ball, use the Sobolev gluing property across the boundary, and cancel the identical exterior energies. We retain the normalization <a id="eq:monotone-tension"></a>$$s_0=\int_{-1}^1\sqrt{2W(a)}\,\mathrm da
     =\int_\mathbb Rg'(s)^2\,\mathrm ds
     =\frac{2\sqrt2}{3}=\frac\sigma2.
 \label{eq:monotone-tension}$$

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
###### Stable end profiles and a variational comparison

The first task is to turn strict monotonicity into minimizing barriers. The positive directional derivative gives stability, and a long-cylinder test transfers that stability to the seven-dimensional limits. Their classification then supplies a calibration, even when a limit is a planar profile rather than a constant well.

<a id="stmt-lem-monotone-ends"></a>
<div id="lem:monotone-ends" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:monotone-end-convergence"></a>
**Lemma 4** (End profiles). *Let $u\in C^2(\mathbb R^8,(-1,1))$ solve $\Delta u=u^3-u$ and satisfy $u_8>0$. There are smooth stable solutions $v_-,v_+:\mathbb R^7\to[-1,1]$ such that $$u(x',t+s)\longrightarrow v_\pm(x')
 \quad\text{in }C^k_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^8)
 \quad\text{as }s\to\pm\infty,
 \qquad k\ge0.
 \label{eq:monotone-end-convergence}$$ They satisfy $v_-(x')<u(x',t)<v_+(x')$ everywhere. Their extensions independent of the eighth coordinate are global minimizers in $\mathbb R^8$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-monotone-ends"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-monotone-ends</summary>

*Proof.* Elliptic interior estimates, applied on unit balls, give smoothness and uniform bounds for every fixed derivative order of $u$. The function $h=u_8>0$ satisfies $(-\Delta+3u^2-1)h=0$. For every compactly supported $C^1$ function $\eta$, integration by parts gives the ground-state identity <a id="eq:monotone-ground-state"></a>$$\int_{\mathbb R^8}\bigl(|\nabla\eta|^2+(3u^2-1)\eta^2\bigr)
   =\int_{\mathbb R^8}h^2\left|\nabla\left(\frac\eta h\right)\right|^2
   \ge0.
 \label{eq:monotone-ground-state}$$ The division is legitimate because $h$ has a positive minimum on the support of $\eta$.

Bounded monotonicity gives pointwise limits along each vertical line. Translation compactness and the derivative bounds improve this to <a href="#eq:monotone-end-convergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:monotone-end-convergence">[eq:monotone-end-convergence]</a>. Indeed every convergent subsequence has the same pointwise limit, and a bounded vertical shift leaves that limit unchanged. Passing the equation and stability to these limits first gives solutions and stability in eight dimensions. To see stability in seven dimensions, fix $\varphi\in C_c^1(\mathbb R^7)$ and a nonzero $\zeta\in C_c^1(\mathbb R)$. Test with $\eta(x',t)=\varphi(x')\zeta(t/L)$ and divide by $L\int\zeta^2$. The result is $$\int_{\mathbb R^7}\bigl(|\nabla\varphi|^2+(3v_\pm^2-1)\varphi^2\bigr)
 \ge -\frac1{L^2}\frac{\int|\zeta'|^2}{\int\zeta^2}
                         \int_{\mathbb R^7}\varphi^2.$$ Letting $L\to\infty$ proves the assertion. For example, $u(x',t)<u(x',t+1)\le v_+(x')$, which proves the strict upper inequality; the lower one is analogous.

Theorem <a href="#thm:stable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:stable">2</a> says that each $v_\pm$ is a constant well or a planar heteroclinic. To check the minimizing assertion directly, put $\Phi(a)=\int_{-1}^a\sqrt{2W(b)}\,\mathrm db$. For a unit vector $e$ and a competitor $w\in H^1(\Omega,[-1,1])$, <a id="eq:monotone-calibration"></a>$$\frac12|\nabla w|^2+W(w)
    \ge \sqrt{2W(w)}\,\partial_e w
    =\partial_e\Phi(w).
 \label{eq:monotone-calibration}$$ There is equality when $w(x)=g(e\cdot x-c)$, since $g'=\sqrt{2W(g)}$. The integral of the last expression depends only on the boundary trace. Thus such a profile minimizes on every ball, in any ambient dimension in which $e$ is a unit vector. This includes the extensions of the seven-dimensional profiles, with $e_8=0$. The constants $\pm1$ have zero energy and also minimize. ◻

</details>

The next comparison also applies in other dimensions. It records exactly which property of the end profiles is used. The constrained comparison between end profiles was proved by Alberti, Ambrosio and Cabré (Alberti, Ambrosio, and Cabré 2001, Theorem 4.4) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[AlbertiAmbrosioCabre2001](#ref-AlbertiAmbrosioCabre2001)]. The unrestricted minimization implication is the classical theorem of Jerison and Monneau (2004, Theorem 1.3, p. 441; proof on pp. 452–453) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[JerisonMonneau2004](#ref-JerisonMonneau2004)]; we include the compact comparison argument in the present normalization. Its globally $C^{1,1}$ potential hypothesis is met by $$\widetilde W(s)=
 \begin{cases}
  (s+1)^2,&s<-1,\\
  W(s),&-1\le s\le1,\\
  (s-1)^2,&s>1.
 \end{cases}$$ The values and the first two derivatives match at the wells, and $\widetilde W'$ is globally Lipschitz. Both potentials are nonnegative and agree on $[-1,1]$. Clipping any competitor to this interval decreases its gradient energy and replaces its exterior potential by zero for either potential, so their minimizing comparisons for $u$ agree.

<a id="stmt-lem-monotone-minimizer"></a>
<div id="lem:monotone-minimizer" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:monotone-energy-growth"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Minimizing barriers and compact sliding). *Let $N\ge2$, and suppose a bounded solution $u:\mathbb R^{N-1}\times\mathbb R\to(-1,1)$ of $\Delta u=W'(u)$ is strictly increasing in the last coordinate. Suppose its vertical limits $a_\pm(x')$, extended to $\mathbb R^N$, are global minimizers. Then $u$ is a global minimizer. Moreover there is a dimensional constant $C_N$ such that $$\mathcal E(u;B_R(y))\le C_N R^{N-1}
 \qquad (y\in\mathbb R^N,\ R\ge2).
 \label{eq:monotone-energy-growth}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-monotone-minimizer"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-monotone-minimizer</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a ball $B$. The direct method produces an energy minimizer $w$ with boundary trace $u$; truncation allows $|w|\le1$. First clip it from below by $a_-$, writing $w_1=\max\{w,a_-\}$. The Sobolev lattice identity is $$\mathcal E(\max\{w,a_-\};B)
 +\mathcal E(\min\{w,a_-\};B)
 =\mathcal E(w;B)+\mathcal E(a_-;B).$$ Since $u>a_-$ on $\partial B$, the minimum on the left has the same trace as $a_-$. Minimality of $a_-$ therefore shows $\mathcal E(w_1;B)\le\mathcal E(w;B)$. The maximum has trace $u$, so it remains an unconstrained minimizer. The analogous upper clipping gives another unconstrained minimizer $\widetilde w=\min\{w_1,a_+\}$ satisfying $a_-\le\widetilde w\le a_+$.

The Euler–Lagrange equation and elliptic boundary regularity make $\widetilde w$ smooth up to $\partial B$. A difference of two solutions $b,c$ here satisfies $-\Delta(b-c)+(b^2+bc+c^2-1)(b-c)=0$, with bounded zeroth-order coefficient. Strong comparison for a nonnegative solution of this linear equation, together with the strict boundary inequalities, gives $$a_-<\widetilde w<a_+\quad\text{on }\overline B.$$ These gaps have positive minima on this compact set. Vertical translates $u_s(x',t)=u(x',t+s)$ converge uniformly there to $a_+$ as $s\to+\infty$, so $u_s>\widetilde w$ for all sufficiently large $s$. Let $$s_* =\inf\{s\ge0:u_s\ge\widetilde w\text{ on }\overline B\}.$$ The set inside the infimum is an upper interval, by strict monotonicity. Continuity gives $u_{s_*}\ge\widetilde w$. If $s_*>0$, then $u_{s_*}>u=\widetilde w$ on $\partial B$, and strong comparison gives strict inequality throughout $\overline B$. Compactness and continuity then allow a smaller positive translation, a contradiction. Thus $\widetilde w\le u$. For the reverse inequality, $u_{-s}$ lies below $\widetilde w$ when $s$ is large. Decrease $s\ge0$ until first contact. A positive first-contact parameter is again impossible, because $u_{-s}<u=\widetilde w$ on $\partial B$ and strong comparison gives a strict compact gap. At $s=0$ this yields $u\le\widetilde w$. Consequently $u=\widetilde w$ on $B$, and $B$ was arbitrary.

Finally choose a smooth cutoff $\eta_R$ that is zero on $B_{R-1}(y)$, one near $\partial B_R(y)$, and satisfies $|\nabla\eta_R|\le C$. The competitor $1+\eta_R(u-1)$ has zero energy in the inner ball and bounded energy density in the remaining shell. The latter assertion uses $|u|\le1$ and the uniform unit-scale interior gradient estimate for the equation. The shell has volume at most $C_NR^{N-1}$, proving <a href="#eq:monotone-energy-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:monotone-energy-growth">[eq:monotone-energy-growth]</a>. ◻

</details>

Lemmas <a href="#lem:monotone-ends" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:monotone-ends">4</a> and <a href="#lem:monotone-minimizer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:monotone-minimizer">5</a> apply to the solution of Theorem <a href="#thm:monotone" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:monotone">1</a>. They establish minimization and the required energy growth directly from its hypotheses and Theorem <a href="#thm:stable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:stable">2</a>.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
###### Exact convergence to a minimizing boundary

We have obtained the variational comparison needed for the blow-down. The remaining issue in this step is equality of measures: phase convergence and a perimeter lower bound alone would permit additional diffuse energy. We eliminate that possibility by comparing with a recovery sequence patched to the original solution in a thin annular layer. This argument does not require a prescribed trace for the recovery sequence.

<a id="eq:monotone-recovery"></a>
We use the following established variational and geometric inputs. For a finite-perimeter set $F$ in a bounded Lipschitz domain $\Omega$, the Modica–Mortola recovery theorem provides, for any sequence $\varepsilon_j\downarrow0$, functions $w_j\in H^1(\Omega,[-1,1])$ such that $$\begin{split}
 w_j&\longrightarrow 2\mathbf1_F-1\quad\text{in }L^1(\Omega),\\
 \limsup_j\int_\Omega
   \left(\frac{\varepsilon_j}2|\nabla w_j|^2+\frac{W(w_j)}{\varepsilon_j}\right)
   &\le s_0 P(F;\Omega).
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:monotone-recovery}$$ Here $P(F;\Omega)=|D\mathbf1_F|(\Omega)$ is relative perimeter (Modica and Mortola 1977) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[ModicaMortola1977](#ref-ModicaMortola1977)]; the finite-perimeter recovery statement is also proved in Modica (1987, sec. 3, pp. 140–141) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Modica1987](#ref-Modica1987)]. For a locally perimeter-minimizing set in $\mathbb R^N$, its perimeter measure defines a stationary integral varifold with multiplicity one on the reduced boundary. Its support is a smooth embedded minimal hypersurface outside a closed singular set of Hausdorff dimension at most $N-8$ (and the singular set is empty when $N\le7$). The centered area ratio is nondecreasing, and equality at all scales forces a cone. These are the classical regularity, dimension-reduction, and monotonicity results for minimizing boundaries (Federer 1969; Giusti 1984; Simons 1968) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Federer1969](#ref-Federer1969), [Giusti1984](#ref-Giusti1984), [Simons1968](#ref-Simons1968)]; for the sharp singular-set dimension bound, see Federer (1970, Theorem 1, p. 769) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Federer1970](#ref-Federer1970)]. The argument below uses recovery and comparison to prove exact energy convergence before using this multiplicity-one geometric theory.

<a id="stmt-prop-monotone-exact-limit"></a>
<div id="prop:monotone-exact-limit" class="proposition">

**Proposition 6** (Exact perimeter limit). *Let $u:\mathbb R^N\to[-1,1]$ be an entire global minimizer with $\mathcal E(u;B_R)\le CR^{N-1}$ for $R\ge2$. Given $R_j\to\infty$, set $\varepsilon_j=R_j^{-1}$ and $u_j(x)=u(R_jx)$. After taking a subsequence there is a locally finite-perimeter set $E$ such that <a id="eq:monotone-exact-limit"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 u_j&\longrightarrow 2\mathbf1_E-1
                    &&\text{in }L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^N),\\
 \left(\frac{\varepsilon_j}2|\nabla u_j|^2+
                \frac{W(u_j)}{\varepsilon_j}\right)\,\mathrm dx
   &\rightharpoonup s_0|D\mathbf1_E|
                    &&\text{as local Radon measures}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:monotone-exact-limit}$$ The set $E$ is locally perimeter minimizing against compact perturbations.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-monotone-exact-limit"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-monotone-exact-limit</summary>

<a id="eq:monotone-rescaled-energy"></a>
<a id="eq:monotone-perimeter-lower"></a>
*Proof.* *Compactness and the lower bound.* Recall the phase primitive $\Phi(a)=\int_{-1}^a\sqrt{2W(b)}\,\,\mathrm db$. Denote the rescaled energy by $\mathcal E_j$ and its energy measure by $\mu_j$. Scaling the minimizing comparison shows that $u_j$ minimizes $\mathcal E_j$ against every compact modification. A change of variables gives $$\mu_j(B_r)=R_j^{1-N}\mathcal E(u;B_{R_jr}).
 \label{eq:monotone-rescaled-energy}$$ Thus these measures are locally bounded. Moreover $$|\nabla\Phi(u_j)|
   \le \frac{\varepsilon_j}2|\nabla u_j|^2+\frac{W(u_j)}{\varepsilon_j},
 \qquad \int_K W(u_j)\le\varepsilon_j\mu_j(K)\longrightarrow0$$ on each compact set $K$. Local BV compactness, followed by a diagonal subsequence, implies that $\Phi(u_j)$ has an $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ limit. The vanishing potential forces its values to lie in $\{\Phi(-1),\Phi(1)\}=\{0,s_0\}$. The inverse of $\Phi$ is uniformly continuous on $[0,s_0]$, so this also proves the asserted local $L^1$ convergence of $u_j$ to $2\mathbf1_E-1$. Weak compactness of $\mu_j$ and lower semicontinuity of total variation give a local measure limit $\mu$ satisfying $$\mu\ge s_0|D\mathbf1_E|.
 \label{eq:monotone-perimeter-lower}$$

*A recovery comparison with the correct boundary trace.* We prove the opposite inequality, including the boundary gluing. Fix a ball $B_r(y)$ and $\delta>0$. Let $F$ be a finite-perimeter set in $B_{r+\delta}(y)$ that agrees with $E$ on the annulus $A=B_{r+\delta}(y)\setminus\overline{B_r(y)}$. The case $F=E$ is permitted. Choose a recovery sequence $w_j$ on this larger ball as in <a href="#eq:monotone-recovery" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:monotone-recovery">[eq:monotone-recovery]</a>. Both $w_j$ and $u_j$ converge on $A$ to $2\mathbf1_E-1$. Their boundedness implies <a id="eq:monotone-annulus-mismatch"></a>$$\int_A|u_j-w_j|^2\le2\int_A|u_j-w_j|\longrightarrow0.
 \label{eq:monotone-annulus-mismatch}$$

For $\varepsilon_j<\delta/4$, partition all but a remainder of $A$ into $L_j=\lfloor\delta/\varepsilon_j\rfloor-1$ disjoint radial layers of thickness $\varepsilon_j$. Thus $L_j\ge\delta/(2\varepsilon_j)$, and the outer remainder leaves a neighborhood of $\partial B_{r+\delta}(y)$ untouched. In one such layer $S$, let $\chi$ increase from zero to one, with $|\nabla\chi|\le C/\varepsilon_j$, and interpolate by $z_j=(1-\chi)w_j+\chi u_j$. The cutoff is zero on the inner side and one on the outer side. On $[-1,1]$, boundedness of $W''$ and $|W'|^2\le CW$ give, uniformly for $0\le\chi\le1$, $$W((1-\chi)a+\chi b)
       \le C\bigl(W(a)+W(b)+|a-b|^2\bigr).$$ Expanding the gradient therefore yields <a id="eq:monotone-strip-cost"></a>$$\mathcal E_j(z_j;S)
 \le C\bigl(\mathcal E_j(u_j;S)+\mathcal E_j(w_j;S)\bigr)
             +\frac C{\varepsilon_j}\int_S|u_j-w_j|^2.
 \label{eq:monotone-strip-cost}$$ Averaging the right-hand side over the disjoint layers selects $S_j$ with cost at most <a id="eq:monotone-strip-average"></a>$$C\frac{\varepsilon_j}{\delta}
     \bigl(\mathcal E_j(u_j;A)+\mathcal E_j(w_j;A)\bigr)
       +\frac C\delta\int_A|u_j-w_j|^2=o_j(1).
 \label{eq:monotone-strip-average}$$ Here $\delta$ is fixed before $j\to\infty$; the two energies are bounded by compactness and recovery. In particular no rate for the $L^1$ convergence is needed: the averaging cancels the factor $\varepsilon_j^{-1}$ multiplying the mismatch in a single layer.

<a id="eq:monotone-competitor-bound"></a>
Use $w_j$ inside $S_j$, the interpolation on $S_j$, and $u_j$ outside $S_j$. This is an admissible compact modification of $u_j$. Its minimizing property, and nonnegativity of the original energy in the part of the annulus inside $S_j$, give $$\mathcal E_j(u_j;B_r(y))
   \le \mathcal E_j(w_j;B_{r+\delta}(y))+o_j(1).$$ Consequently, whenever $\mu(\partial B_r(y))=0$, $$\mu(B_r(y))\le s_0 P(F;B_{r+\delta}(y)).
 \label{eq:monotone-competitor-bound}$$ *Equality of energy and perimeter, then minimality.* First set $F=E$. At radii with $\mu(\partial B_r(y))=|D\mathbf1_E|(\partial B_r(y))=0$, send $\delta\downarrow0$ in <a href="#eq:monotone-competitor-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:monotone-competitor-bound">[eq:monotone-competitor-bound]</a>. These are all but countably many radii at each center. Together with <a href="#eq:monotone-perimeter-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:monotone-perimeter-lower">[eq:monotone-perimeter-lower]</a>, the resulting inequalities on balls prove $\mu=s_0|D\mathbf1_E|$ as measures. More explicitly, $\mu-s_0|D\mathbf1_E|$ is already a nonnegative Radon measure by <a href="#eq:monotone-perimeter-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:monotone-perimeter-lower">[eq:monotone-perimeter-lower]</a>, and it vanishes on these balls, which form a neighborhood basis. In particular no additional transition-layer mass remains.

Next suppose $F\mathbin\triangle E$ is compactly contained in an open ball. Choose $r$ outside that compact set, inside the ball, and with zero perimeter measure on $\partial B_r(y)$. Then $F=E$ near this sphere, so it is a continuity sphere for both perimeter measures. Apply <a href="#eq:monotone-competitor-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:monotone-competitor-bound">[eq:monotone-competitor-bound]</a> and let $\delta\downarrow0$ to obtain $P(E;B_r(y))\le P(F;B_r(y))$. The perimeter measures coincide outside the compact modification; hence this proves local perimeter minimality, as asserted. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
###### The directed minimizing cone in dimension eight

The exact limit is now available together with perimeter minimality. We next combine energy monotonicity with vertical monotonicity: the first makes the limiting boundary a cone, while the second fixes the sign of its vertical normal component. In this geometric step, dimension eight enters when we analyze the singular set of this cone.

<a id="eq:monotone-cone-ratios"></a>
For our eight-dimensional solution, the gradient inequality <a href="#eq:diffuse-modica" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:diffuse-modica">[eq:diffuse-modica]</a> and monotonicity formula <a href="#eq:finite-monotonicity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-monotonicity">[eq:finite-monotonicity]</a> give a nondecreasing normalized energy $$M_r(u)=\frac{\mathcal E(u;B_r)}{s_0\omega_7r^7}.$$ By <a href="#eq:monotone-energy-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:monotone-energy-growth">[eq:monotone-energy-growth]</a> it has a finite limit $M_\infty$. This limit is positive, since $u$ is nonconstant and its energy is positive on some ball. Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:monotone-exact-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:monotone-exact-limit">6</a> to any sequence $R_j\to\infty$. At continuity radii, its exact convergence and <a href="#eq:monotone-rescaled-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:monotone-rescaled-energy">[eq:monotone-rescaled-energy]</a> imply $$P(E;B_r)=\omega_7r^7M_\infty.
 \label{eq:monotone-cone-ratios}$$ Squeezing between continuity radii proves this identity for every $r>0$. Thus the stationary varifold of the minimizing boundary has constant centered area ratio. The equality case of monotonicity makes its support $$C=\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}|D\mathbf1_E|$$ a cone with vertex at the origin. It is nonempty and contains the origin. Its multiplicity is one, since its weight is the perimeter measure.

Vertical monotonicity also survives this convergence. For each nonnegative $\psi\in C_c^1(\mathbb R^8)$, $$-\int u_j\,\partial_8\psi=\int (\partial_8u_j)\psi\ge0.$$ The $L^1$ limit gives $\partial_8\mathbf1_E\ge0$ as a distribution and hence as a measure. Let $\nu_E$ denote the unit normal pointing *into* $E$ on the regular boundary, so that $D\mathbf1_E=\nu_E|D\mathbf1_E|$. Then <a id="eq:monotone-directed-cone"></a>$$\nu_E\cdot e_8\ge0\quad\text{on the regular part of }C.
 \label{eq:monotone-directed-cone}$$

<a id="stmt-lem-monotone-flat-cone"></a>
<div id="lem:monotone-flat-cone" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Flatness of the directed cone). *Let $E\subset\mathbb R^8$ be locally perimeter minimizing. Suppose its nonempty boundary support $C$ is a cone with vertex $0$ and $\partial_8\mathbf1_E\ge0$. Then $C$ is a hyperplane, with perimeter multiplicity one.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-monotone-flat-cone"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-monotone-flat-cone</summary>

*Proof.* This is the classical directed-cone reduction; see also Schnürer (2003, Theorem 4.5 and Corollary 4.6) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Schnuerer2003](#ref-Schnuerer2003)]. We give its short proof to make the treatment of the vertex explicit. *The cone has a smooth compact link.* The singular-set dimension bound is at most zero. A nonzero singular point would, by dilation invariance, generate a whole singular ray: each dilation is an ambient diffeomorphism preserving $C$, and therefore preserves the distinction between smooth and singular boundary points. This is impossible, so $\operatorname{Sing}C\subset\{0\}$. Consequently $$\Lambda=C\cap\mathbb S^7$$ is a smooth compact embedded minimal hypersurface of $\mathbb S^7$, possibly with several connected components. The oriented normal is continuous on the regular boundary. Since tangent normal lines are dilation invariant and the choice of side is continuous, that orientation is constant along every regular ray: it takes values in the same two-point set along that connected ray. This uses conicality of the support and does not presume conicality of $E$. The function $f=\nu_E\cdot e_8\ge0$ is therefore homogeneous of degree zero.

<a id="eq:monotone-link-jacobi"></a>
The normal component of a Euclidean translation on a minimal hypersurface satisfies the Jacobi equation (Simons 1968, Corollary 3.3.1, p. 74) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Simons1968](#ref-Simons1968)] $$\Delta_C f+|A_C|^2f=0.$$ The cone metric is $\,\mathrm dr^2+r^2g_\Lambda$ and $|A_C|^2=r^{-2}|A_\Lambda|^2$, where $A_\Lambda$ is the second fundamental form of $\Lambda$ in the sphere. Thus $$\Delta_\Lambda f+|A_\Lambda|^2f=0.
 \label{eq:monotone-link-jacobi}$$ We separate the case where this nonnegative function is positive on a link component from the case where it vanishes on every component. *A positive directional normal component.* On a connected component where $f$ is not identically zero, the strong maximum principle gives $f>0$. Integration of <a href="#eq:monotone-link-jacobi" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:monotone-link-jacobi">[eq:monotone-link-jacobi]</a> on that compact component yields $\int|A_\Lambda|^2f=0$, so it is totally geodesic. A connected compact embedded totally geodesic hypersurface of the sphere is a whole equatorial $\mathbb S^6$. Any other component, being disjoint from this equator, would lie in one of its open hemispheres. This cannot happen: the corresponding signed coordinate function $\ell$ on a closed minimal six-dimensional hypersurface of $\mathbb S^7$ satisfies $\Delta_\Lambda\ell+6\ell=0$, hence has integral zero, whereas in an open hemisphere it has strict constant sign. There are consequently no other components, and $C$ is the hyperplane over that equator.

*A vanishing directional normal component.* It remains to handle the case $f\equiv0$ on all of $\Lambda$. Perimeter measure gives zero mass to the possible singular point, so now <a id="eq:monotone-vertical-invariance"></a>$$\partial_8\mathbf1_E=0\quad\text{on all of }\mathbb R^8.
 \label{eq:monotone-vertical-invariance}$$ A locally integrable function whose distributional derivative in one direction vanishes is invariant under translations in that direction. For example this follows by mollifying, applying the assertion to the smooth functions, and passing to the local $L^1$ limit. Thus each vertical translation preserves $\mathbf1_E$ as an $L^1_{\rm loc}$ class, and also preserves its perimeter measure, its support $C$, and the singular set of that support. If $0$ were singular, every point of the vertical axis would be singular, contradicting $\operatorname{Sing}C\subset\{0\}$. Therefore the origin is regular. A cone smooth at its vertex equals its tangent hyperplane there: dilating toward the origin keeps the cone fixed while its smooth local graphs converge to that tangent plane. This proves flatness in the remaining case. In either case the weight is $\mathcal H^7\mathbin\llcorner C$, by the multiplicity-one structure of a minimizing boundary. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:monotone-unit-density"></a>
Applying Lemma <a href="#lem:monotone-flat-cone" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:monotone-flat-cone">7</a> to <a href="#eq:monotone-cone-ratios" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:monotone-cone-ratios">[eq:monotone-cone-ratios]</a> shows $$M_\infty(u)=1.
 \label{eq:monotone-unit-density}$$ This is the point where the exact measure convergence in Proposition <a href="#prop:monotone-exact-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:monotone-exact-limit">6</a> is needed: flat support alone would not determine the diffuse energy density.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
###### Return to the equation and the exact profile

The near-unit-density theorem of Wang (Wang 2017, Theorem 11.1, p. 3036) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[Wang2017](#ref-Wang2017)] says that, in every ambient dimension $N$, a nonconstant bounded entire Allen–Cahn solution with the Modica bound and $$\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal E(u;B_R)}
                           {s_0\omega_{N-1}R^{N-1}}
            \le1+\delta_N$$ for a sufficiently small dimensional $\delta_N>0$ is one-dimensional. The constant wells can be included as the separate trivial alternatives; see also Florit-Simon and Serra (2025, Theorem 2.16) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[FSS2025](#ref-FSS2025)]. Our solution has the required range and Modica bound, is nonconstant by $u_8>0$, and satisfies this energy condition by <a href="#eq:monotone-unit-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:monotone-unit-density">[eq:monotone-unit-density]</a>. Hence $u(x)=h(e\cdot x)$ for some unit vector $e$.

For completeness we recover the profile and its orientation directly. Since $u_8=e_8h'>0$, we have $e_8\ne0$; reversing $e$ and the scalar coordinate if necessary gives $e_8>0$ and $h'>0$ everywhere. The finite limits $a_\pm=\lim_{s\to\pm\infty}h(s)$ are distinct and lie in $[-1,1]$. Translated compactness for the ODE $h''=h^3-h$ shows $a_\pm^3-a_\pm=0$ and $h'(s)\to0$ at both ends. Indeed any translated subsequential limit at an end is the constant $a_\pm$, and the uniform derivative bounds give convergence of its derivatives. The first integral is $$\frac12h'(s)^2-W(h(s))=\text{constant}.$$ Taking both limits gives $W(a_-)=W(a_+)$. Among the three stationary roots $-1,0,1$, the only distinct pair with equal potential is $-1,1$. Thus $a_-=-1$, $a_+=1$, and the first integral and $h'>0$ give $$h'=\frac{1-h^2}{\sqrt2},\qquad
 h(s)=\tanh\left(\frac{s-c}{\sqrt2}\right)$$ for some $c\in\mathbb R$. This proves Theorem <a href="#thm:monotone" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:monotone">1</a>, including the condition $e_8>0$. Conversely this formula has strict range $(-1,1)$, satisfies $g''=g^3-g$, and obeys $u_8=e_8g'(e\cdot x-c)>0$.

<a id="sec:classical-estimates"></a>
<a id="sec:diffuse"></a>
<a id="sec:dimension-nine"></a>
<a id="sec:interface"></a>
### Stable solutions with finite density

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
##### Guide to the analytical proof

This section explains how the three analytical parts fit together. It may be skipped on a first reading: the definitions and proofs begin afresh in Section <a href="#sec:diffuse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:diffuse">2</a>.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
###### Finite density and the diffuse defect

The main quantities measure departure from a planar transition. For $|v|<1$, put $$q=1-v^2,\qquad U=g^{-1}(v),\qquad D=1-|\nabla U|^2.$$ Modica’s gradient bound gives $D\ge0$ (Modica 1985) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[Modica1985](#ref-Modica1985)]. A planar transition has affine $U$ and $D=0$. For a stable solution the amplitude $q$ gives the exact identity <a id="eq:intro-defect-test"></a>$$\label{eq:intro-defect-test}
 0\le Q_v(q\eta)
   =\int_{\mathbb R^n}q^2|\nabla\eta|^2\,\mathrm dx
     -\int_{\mathbb R^n}q^3D\eta^2\,\mathrm dx
 \qquad(\eta\in C_c^1(\mathbb R^n)).$$ The inverse-profile formulation and this test also occur in Liu et al. (2026, Lemma 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[LLWWW2026](#ref-LLWWW2026)]; we derive them in the present dimension. The second term detects defect, including at zeros where a smooth level-set description is unavailable.

At a regular point of $\Sigma=\{v=0\}$, let $\nu=\nabla U/|\nabla U|$ be the normal pointing toward increasing $v$, let $A=d\nu|_{T\Sigma}$ be the second fundamental form, and set $H=\operatorname{tr}A$. Small defect gives a local description by one nearly planar transition. For its normal averages the appropriate normalization is $$G(s)=1-g(s)^2,\qquad
 \sigma=\int_\mathbb RG(s)^2\,\mathrm ds=\frac{4\sqrt2}{3}=2s_0.$$ For a fixed, sufficiently large normal length $P$, define <a id="eq:intro-normal-average"></a>$$\label{eq:intro-normal-average}
 I_P(z)=\frac1\sigma\int_{-P}^{P}
 q(z+s\nu(z))^3D(z+s\nu(z))\,\mathrm ds.$$ This is an average along a normal segment, not the pointwise defect $D(z)$. We prove that $I_P$ controls both curvature and mean curvature, with a sharp coefficient in the latter bound. The limiting equation obtained by normalizing $D$ by its value $D(z_j)\to0$ at a zero has a nonnegative source even in the normal coordinate. Separating this source from the positive homogeneous solutions gives the strict coefficient needed when the interface has dimension six.

<a id="eq:intro-surface-stability"></a>
For the later unbounded-density argument in dimension seven, we will also need a surface inequality. Lifting a surface test into the ambient equation yields $$\label{eq:intro-surface-stability}
 (1-\varepsilon)\int_\Sigma
       \frac{|A|^2+I_P}{2}\psi^2\,\mathrm dS
