For any lifted amplitude $G f$, expand its normal derivative and integrate the cross term $2GG'ff_sJ$ by parts. Using $G''=(3g^2-1)G$ gives precisely <a href="#eq:L4" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L4">[eq:L4]</a>, with $f=\beta\psi$. There are no artificial chart boundaries in this computation: the amplitude is globally defined on the lifted surface, while local inverse charts are used only to justify its pushdown. Finite even normal caps may be removed whenever the displayed terms are dominated by a polynomial in $|s|$ times $G^2$; this is the case in the applications below. At $a=0$ take $\beta=1$ and use the same capped limit. ◻

</details>

Physical stability and the variance identity show that the lifted quadratic form is nonnegative. We may discard the subtracted variance for the next estimate; Section <a href="#sec:growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:growth">3</a> will retain it in a more general gluing construction. The next proposition combines two negative terms in the lifted quadratic form. The normal Jacobian supplies $a^2/2$, while the profile deficit supplies $I_P/2$. Keeping both terms will allow separate control of curvature and of interaction in the later estimates.

<a id="stmt-prop-interface-strengthened-stability"></a>
<div id="prop:interface-strengthened-stability" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:L3"></a>
**Proposition 31** (Strengthened surface stability). *For each $\varepsilon>0$, choose $P$ sufficiently large as in Lemma <a href="#lem:interface-sharp-means" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:interface-sharp-means">28</a>. There exists $\delta>0$ such that every compactly supported Lipschitz function on $\{z\in\Sigma: D(z)<\delta\}$ satisfies $$\label{eq:L3}
 (1-\varepsilon)\int_\Sigma\frac{a^2+I_P}{2}\psi^2\,\,\mathrm dS
 \le\int_\Sigma|\nabla_\Sigma\psi|^2\,\,\mathrm dS.$$ In the lifted computation the gradient coefficient can be made arbitrarily close to $\sigma$, and the negative coefficient arbitrarily close to $\sigma(a^2+I_P)/2$, with the same choices.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-interface-strengthened-stability"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-interface-strengthened-stability</summary>

*Proof.* Use Lemma <a href="#lem:interface-immersed-test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:interface-immersed-test">30</a>. On the support of $\beta$ the pullback tangential metric differs from the surface metric by $O(\alpha)$. Uniform derivative estimates give $|\nabla\beta|\le C_\alpha(1+|s|)$; derivatives of $a=|A|$ are understood weakly at its zero set. All terms containing a derivative of $\beta$ are bounded, after a Young inequality with any fixed small parameter, by $C_\alpha e^{-c_\alpha/a}\psi^2$. Thus the gradient part is at most $$(1+C\alpha+\eta)\sigma|\nabla_\Sigma\psi|^2
       +C_{\alpha,\eta}e^{-c_\alpha/a}\psi^2.$$ The exponential remainder is $o(D)$ uniformly as $D\to0$, since $a^2\le CD$.

The elementary-symmetric expansion of $J$ and evenness give $$-\int\beta^2J_sGG'\,\,\mathrm ds
   =\frac\sigma2(H^2-a^2)+o(D).$$ Indeed the constant term $H$ in $J_s$ integrates to zero, the linear term is $(H^2-a^2)s$, and $-\int sGG'=\sigma/2$. Terms of order at least two in $sA$ are either odd under the integral or bounded by $Ca^3=o(D)$. The cap errors have the preceding exponential bound, and $H^2=o(D)$.

<a id="eq:interface-normal-accuracy"></a>
The potential contribution is nonpositive everywhere: $U(z)=0$ and $|\nabla U|\le1$ imply $|U(z+s\nu)|\le|s|$ and hence $v(z+s\nu)^2\le g(s)^2$. We quantify its gain. On the two normal rays define $U_\pm(r)=\pm U(z\pm r\nu)$ for $r\ge0$. Then $$r-U_\pm(r)\ge\frac12\int_0^rD(z\pm t\nu)\,\,\mathrm dt.$$ Indeed $U_\pm'(r)=p(z\pm r\nu)\cdot\nu
\le\sqrt{1-D(z\pm r\nu)}\le1-D(z\pm r\nu)/2$. This writes the same deficit inequality on both sides without changing its sign convention. Moreover, on every fixed segment, $$\label{eq:interface-normal-accuracy}
 |p(z+s\nu)-\nu|+|U(z+s\nu)-s|\le C_P D(z),\qquad |s|\le P.$$ To prove it, initially $|p(z)-\nu|\le CD(z)$, and along the segment $$|T\nu|\le|Tp|+|T||p-\nu|
       \le C_P D(z)+C_P\sqrt{D(z)}|p-\nu|.$$ A Gronwall estimate gives <a href="#eq:interface-normal-accuracy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interface-normal-accuracy">[eq:interface-normal-accuracy]</a>. Here $Tp=-\nabla D/2$ and <a href="#eq:diffuse-local" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:diffuse-local">[eq:diffuse-local]</a> were used.

For a fixed segment $[-Q,Q]$, <a href="#eq:interface-normal-accuracy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interface-normal-accuracy">[eq:interface-normal-accuracy]</a> gives $p\cdot\nu>0$ once the threshold is small enough. Thus $U(s)$ has the sign of $s$ on both half-segments. After division by $D(z)$ and passage to a normalized limit, the potential gain $3(g^2-v^2)G^2$ is at least $$-\frac12(G^3)'(s)\int_0^s\mathcal D(0,t)\,\,\mathrm dt.$$ This expression is nonnegative on both half-lines. Integrating and using Tonelli, its total mass is $\tfrac12\int_\mathbb RG^3\mathcal D\,\,\mathrm ds$. To make the finite-window order explicit, first fix $P$ and put $h_P(s)=\int_0^s\mathbf1_{\{|t|\le P\}}\mathcal D(0,t)\,\,\mathrm dt$. For $Q\ge P$, integration by parts gives exactly $$-\frac12\int_{-Q}^{Q}(G^3)'h_P\,\,\mathrm ds
 =\frac12\int_{-P}^{P}G^3\mathcal D\,\,\mathrm ds
       -\frac{G(Q)^3}{2}\int_{-P}^{P}\mathcal D\,\,\mathrm ds.$$ Local Harnack bounds the last integral by $C_P$, uniformly for normalized limits. Choose $Q$ large after $P$ so the loss is as small as prescribed, then decrease the defect threshold to use compactness on $[-Q,Q]$. This threshold also ensures $\beta=1$ on that segment. Since $I_P/D\ge c$, for any $\eta>0$ this gives $$\int 3(g^2-v(\Phi)^2)G^2\beta^2J\,\,\mathrm ds
       \ge(1-\eta)\frac\sigma2 I_P$$ when the defect threshold is sufficiently small. Fix $\eta$, then $\alpha$ and the threshold, in that order, to absorb the $o(D)$ terms using $I_P\ge cD$. Physical stability and the upper bound by the lifted form now prove <a href="#eq:L3" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L3">[eq:L3]</a>. All normal intervals used here are fixed before the final defect threshold: choose $P$, then the larger extraction interval $Q$, and then decrease the threshold. No later surface collar or surface-test amplitude enters those choices. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-48"></a>
###### Comparison at the exact exponential rate

For $0<\delta\le1/4$ set $$X=\{z\in\Sigma:D(z)\ge\delta\},\qquad
 \bar a(z)=\begin{cases}1,&z\in X,\\ a(z),&z\notin X,\end{cases}
 \qquad \kappa=\frac{\sqrt2}{G_1},\qquad E(z)=e^{-\kappa d(z)}.$$ The zero set is closed, and $X$ includes every singular zero. Here $E$ records the interaction scale associated with reach. To compare it with $D$, we must also retain curvature away from the base: a local curvature value alone does not control the two-sided comparison problem. The exponentially weighted supremum $J_0$ in the next proposition records that curvature, with value $1$ assigned to the excluded set $X$.

<a id="stmt-prop-interface-exact-comparison"></a>
<div id="prop:interface-exact-comparison" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:L5"></a>
**Proposition 32** (Exact exponential comparison). *There exist $\lambda>0$ and $C<\infty$, depending on the fixed penalty and dimension but independent of $\delta\in(0,1/4]$, such that $$\label{eq:L5}
 D(z)\le C\bigl(J_0(z)+E(z)\bigr),\qquad
 E(z)\le C\bigl(J_0(z)+D(z)\bigr),\qquad z\in\Sigma\setminus X,
 \quad J_0(z)=\sup_{y\in\Sigma}e^{-\lambda|y-z|}\bar a(y)^2.$$*

</div>

We prove the proposition with explicit comparisons. In particular, no loss in the exponent $\sqrt2$ is absorbed into a constant. The lower bound for $D$ comes from a positive kernel and the opposite sheet. For the upper bound, we construct an isolated solution on each side of the same base graph. Adding their equations cancels the terms linear in the graph’s mean curvature. The remaining task is to compare each artificial solution with the true phase while preserving the exact exponential rate across a growing chamber.

<a id="stmt-lem-interface-halfspace-inverse"></a>
<div id="lem:interface-halfspace-inverse" class="lemma">

**Lemma 33** (A half-space inverse). *Let $p_0>n$, $V_g=3g^2-1$, and $L_g=-\Delta_y-\partial_t^2+V_g(t)$ on $\mathbb R^m\times(0,\infty)$, with zero Dirichlet trace. For some $b_0>0$, uniformly in $0\le b\le b_0$, its inverse maps the norm $$\sup_{(y,t)}e^{-b|y|}\|f\|_{L^{p_0}(B_1(y,t)\cap\{t>0\})}$$ to the analogous uniformly local $W^{2,p_0}$ norm. The inverse is unique in this weighted class.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-interface-halfspace-inverse"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-interface-halfspace-inverse</summary>

*Proof.* Let $v_0$ be the bounded positive half-line function supplied by Lemma <a href="#lem:reach-half-line-barrier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:reach-half-line-barrier">23</a> with initial endpoint $0$. It satisfies $(-\partial_t^2+V_g)v_0=1$, $0<c\le v_0\le C$, and $|v_0'|+|v_0''|\le C$. Its trace at $0$ is positive; the functions to which we apply the inverse, rather than this comparison function, have zero trace. Ground-state substitution, first for compact zero-trace functions, gives $$\int\bigl(|\nabla f|^2+V_g f^2\bigr)
 =\int v_0^2|\nabla(f/v_0)|^2+\int v_0^{-1}f^2
 \ge C^{-1}\|f\|_2^2.$$ The energy inverse therefore exists for compactly supported sources. For a source in a unit block, energy estimates followed by interior and flat-boundary elliptic estimates and Sobolev bootstrapping bound its $W^{2,p_0}$ norm locally by the source’s $L^{p_0}$ norm. These bounds are uniform in the block location; only a fixed number of bootstraps is required because $p_0>n$ is fixed.

Off a fixed enlargement of that block, compare its absolute value with $Cv_0(t)e^{-c_1 r}$, where $r$ is distance to the block center. For sufficiently small fixed $c_1>0$, this is a supersolution: $L_gv_0=1$ dominates the terms bounded by $C(c_1+c_1^2+c_1/r)v_0$. Comparison is valid after division by $v_0$, since the divided operator has positive zeroth coefficient $v_0^{-1}$. On unbounded domains it follows by exhaustion using outer growing exponential barriers; alternatively, the energy solution’s vanishing exterior $L^2$ norm and local estimates give the boundary condition at infinity. Local elliptic estimates then give exponential decay of the $W^{2,p_0}$ norms as well. Partition an arbitrary source into disjoint unit blocks and sum these estimates. The sum converges locally absolutely because $e^{b|y'|}\le e^{b|y|}e^{b|y-y'|}$ and $b<c_1/2$. It provides the stated inverse and norm bound. Here is an explicit uniqueness argument for the full weighted class, including directions with $t\to\infty$. Let $R_*(x)=\sqrt{1+|x|^2}$, and write $C_0=\sup v_0$, $C_1=\sup|v_0'|$. Since $|\nabla R_*|\le1$ and $\Delta R_*\le n$, $$L_g(v_0e^{cR_*})
 \ge e^{cR_*}\bigl[1-2cC_1-ncC_0-c^2C_0\bigr].$$ Choose $c_*>0$ so the bracket is at least $1/2$, and decrease $b_0$ to be at most $\min(c_1/4,c_*/2)$. A homogeneous solution in the weighted class satisfies $|u(y,t)|\le C_u e^{b|y|}$ by local Sobolev embedding. On the hemisphere $|x|=R$, the barrier $\eta v_0e^{c_*R_*}$ dominates $|u|$ for every sufficiently large $R$, since $c_*>b$. On its flat boundary $u=0$. The divided maximum principle on half-balls, for both signs of $u$, therefore gives $|u|\le\eta v_0e^{c_*R_*}$ everywhere. Let $\eta\downarrow0$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-interface-halfspace-inverse-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-interface-halfspace-inverse</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:interface-exact-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:interface-exact-comparison">32</a>.* Fix $z$ and write $B=J_0(z)+E(z)$. We need only treat $B\to0$. For the first inequality other values are trivial. For the second, if $E\le J_0$ there is nothing to prove; if $B$ is bounded below and $E>J_0$, then $d$ is uniformly bounded and $D$ is uniformly bounded below. The latter assertion follows from single-profile compactness: $D(z_j)\to0$ would make $U$ converge to a linear coordinate on every fixed ball, forcing $d(z_j)\to\infty$ by the definition of the penalty. This is also Lemma <a href="#lem:reach-small-defect" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:reach-small-defect">20</a>.

*Step 1: ordered sheets on the comparison scale.* Translate and rotate so $z=0$, $\nu(z)=e_n$. Put $P_0=A_0\log(1/B)$, where $A_0$ will be large and fixed. After $A_0$ is chosen, take $\lambda$ so small that $10\lambda A_0<1/2$. Every zero in $B_{10P_0}$ is then good, and $$|A|^2\le J_0(0)e^{10\lambda P_0}\le B^{1/2}.$$ Indeed a bad zero there would give $J_0(0)\ge B^{10\lambda A_0}>B$. At every such good zero $|\nabla v|\ge\sqrt{3/8}$, so uniform elliptic estimates give uniform graph smoothness on a fixed scale.

The base sheet extends over a disk of radius at least $2P_0$ in its tangent plane. To see this directly, lift radial segments by tangent projection. Along a lift of length $O(P_0)$ the normal changes by at most $O(P_0B^{1/4})=o(1)$, so the projection remains invertible and the lift length remains controlled. All lifts stay inside the regular larger ball, and any terminal limit is regular. Continuation and local uniqueness give a smooth graph; the same argument works for sheets based in a fixed small fraction of $B_{P_0}$. Their heights differ from their tangent planes by $O(P_0^2B^{1/4})=o(1)$.

Here is the ordering argument with the length scales recorded. Fix a small angular parameter $\theta>0$. A sheet based within $\theta P_0$ has unoriented normal within $C\theta+o(1)$ of $e_n$. Otherwise its almost affine disk of radius comparable to $P_0$ crosses the base disk transversally: along a line in the direction of its tilt, its signed height relative to the base changes sign, with magnitude bounded below by a constant times the tilt times $P_0$, while both graph errors are $o(1)$. The intermediate value theorem would give an intersection, impossible for two different regular zero sheets. Thus all pieces in a smaller box are ordered graphs over one horizontal disk. There are finitely many in each closed inner box: infinitely many would accumulate at a regular zero, where the fixed local graph property permits only one piece. Descriptions on overlapping disks agree or are disjoint.

<a id="eq:interface-gap-angle"></a>
Let the first graph above the base have positive-axis intercept less than $\theta P_0/10$, if such a graph exists, and let $\ell$ be its shortest distance to $0$. Its foot normal $n'$ satisfies $$\label{eq:interface-gap-angle}
 |n'-e_n|\le C\frac{\ell+o(1)}{P_0},\qquad
 \{n'\cdot x=\ell\}\text{ is its tangent plane at the foot}.$$ For this stronger bound, apply the preceding nonintersection argument out to the full horizontal scale comparable to $P_0$, now using its height $\ell$ instead of $\theta P_0$. The minimizing foot is interior because the horizontal chart radius greatly exceeds its intercept. Choosing $\theta$ small puts the chord in the constant part of $\Gamma$, and hence $d(0)\le G_1\ell$.

Choose box fractions $c_1=c'_0\theta$, $c_2=c_1/C_0$, with $c'_0$ sufficiently small and $C_0$ sufficiently large dimensional constants. These two constants can be fixed before $\theta$ is decreased further. In $|y|_\infty\le10c_1P_0$ the adjacent height differs from $\ell$ by at most a small fixed fraction of $\ell$, plus $o(1)$, by <a href="#eq:interface-gap-angle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interface-gap-angle">[eq:interface-gap-angle]</a>. This box contains the whole adjacent chamber and ample lateral buffer. If no small intercept exists, the analogous box contains no zero above the base up to height $10c_1P_0$. An intervening zero would extend through the axis to a prohibited smaller intercept. The same statements hold below. All these chamber heights tend to infinity, since $d(0)\ge\kappa^{-1}\log(1/B)$.

<a id="eq:interface-real-gap"></a>
If $E(0)\ge J_0(0)$, then $d(0)=\kappa^{-1}\log(1/E(0))=O(\log(1/B))$. For $A_0$ sufficiently large it is much smaller than $\theta P_0$. Every realizing contact lies in the preceding graph system. A contact on the base sheet, including a diagonal one, has value at least $cB^{-1/4}\gg P_0$, by its curvature bound and the graph Taylor formula. Every other competing sheet has its shortest chord in the plateau, while every chord to it has penalty at least $G_1$. Consequently $$\label{eq:interface-real-gap}
 d(0)=G_1\ell_{\min},$$ where $\ell_{\min}$ is the shortest distance to an adjacent graph. Only adjacent graphs can be shortest, since a segment to a farther graph crosses an intervening zero.

<a id="eq:L6"></a>
*Step 2: the opposite sheet gives the lower interaction bound.* For a fixed unit vector $n_0$, put $w=q(1-p\cdot n_0)\ge0$. The identities for $q$ and $\nabla v$, together with $q p=\sqrt2\nabla v$, give $$\label{eq:L6}
 (-\Delta+2)w=3qw-q^2D\ge qw\ge0.$$ The last inequality uses $D\le2(1-p\cdot n_0)$. The positive kernel of $-\Delta+2$ is $$\mathcal K(x)=\int_0^\infty(4\pi t)^{-n/2}
                       e^{-2t-|x|^2/(4t)}\,\,\mathrm dt.$$ For $r\ge1$ it is bounded above and below by positive constants times $r^{-m/2}e^{-\sqrt2 r}$; the bounds follow by splitting this integral about its minimum at $t=r/(2\sqrt2)$ and scaling the central interval by $\sqrt r$. Convolution represents bounded solutions of this equation, by the maximum principle for their bounded homogeneous difference. In particular a nonnegative subset of its source gives a lower bound.

Take $n_0=e_n$ and assume $E\ge J_0$. On the opposite adjacent sheet realizing <a href="#eq:interface-real-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interface-real-gap">[eq:interface-real-gap]</a>, the oriented normal is almost $-e_n$, because the signs of a continuous function alternate between its regular zero graphs. On a tangent disk of radius $c\sqrt{\ell_{\min}}$ and a fixed thin neighborhood of this disk, $q w$ has a fixed positive lower bound: $D<1/4$ at its zeros and uniform local derivative bounds apply. The distances to $0$ are $\ell_{\min}+O(1)$, and its volume is comparable to $\ell_{\min}^{m/2}$. Thus <a href="#eq:L6" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L6">[eq:L6]</a> gives $$D(0)\ge w(0)\ge c e^{-\sqrt2\ell_{\min}}=cE(0).$$ This proves the second inequality of <a href="#eq:L5" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L5">[eq:L5]</a>, including its exact exponential rate. The remaining inequality requires an upper bound for the deficit of the base slope; a one-sided curvature error would be too large for that purpose.

<a id="eq:L7"></a>
*Step 3: cancellation for two artificial half-space solutions.* Write the base graph as $x_n=h(y)$, with $h(0)=0$ and $\nabla h(0)=0$, and cut it off smoothly outside the preceding comparison region at scale $P_0$. Its extension can be chosen with $$\|h\|_{C^2}=o(1),\qquad
 |\nabla h(y)|+|\nabla^2h(y)|
          \le C\sqrt{J_0(0)}e^{2\lambda|y|}.$$ For an explicit bound independent of $P_0$, take the cutoff equal to $1$ on $|y|\le P_0$ and supported on $|y|\le2P_0$. The uncut graph has $|\nabla h|\le1$, so $|(y,h(y))|\le2|y|$ and $|\nabla^2h(y)|\le C\sqrt{J_0(0)}e^{\lambda|y|}$. Radial integration from $h(0)=\nabla h(0)=0$ gives $$|\nabla h(y)|\le C\lambda^{-1}\sqrt{J_0(0)}e^{\lambda|y|},
 \qquad
 |h(y)|\le C\lambda^{-2}\sqrt{J_0(0)}e^{\lambda|y|}.$$ Derivatives of the cutoff contribute factors $P_0^{-1}$ and $P_0^{-2}$, which are at most $1$ for $P_0\ge1$. Thus the extended graph obeys the displayed weighted estimate with a constant at most $C(1+\lambda^{-1}+\lambda^{-2})$, fixed after $\lambda$, and no factor growing with $\log(1/B)$. Its unweighted $C^2$ smallness follows separately from $P_0^2B^{1/4}\to0$. Use this same extension $h$ on both sides. In the coordinates $x_n=h(y)\pm t$, $t>0$, seek positive solutions $v^*_\pm=g(t)+\varphi_\pm$ with zero boundary. Their equations are $$\label{eq:L7}
 \begin{split}
 L_g\varphi_\pm={}&|\nabla h|^2\partial_{tt}(g+\varphi_\pm)
 \mp2\nabla h\cdot\nabla_y\partial_t\varphi_\pm
 \mp\Delta h\,\partial_t(g+\varphi_\pm)\\
 &-3g\varphi_\pm^2-\varphi_\pm^3.
 \end{split}$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:interface-halfspace-inverse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:interface-halfspace-inverse">33</a> and the embedding $W^{2,p_0}_{\rm uloc}\subset C^{1,\eta}$ make the right-hand side followed by the inverse a contraction in a small unweighted ball. The highest derivative terms have coefficients tending uniformly to zero; the nonlinear terms have Lipschitz constants tending to zero on that ball. The source terms from $g$ tend to zero there. This proves existence and uniqueness of small zero-trace solutions; local elliptic bootstrapping gives smoothness. Weighted estimates in the same equation, with the small-coefficient terms absorbed, then give $$\|\varphi_\pm\|_{W^{2,p_0}(B_1(y,t)\cap\{t>0\})}
       \le C\sqrt{J_0(0)}e^{3\lambda|y|}.$$ They are positive by $C^1$ smallness near $t=0$ and $C^0$ smallness away from it. They are at most $1$: a supremizing sequence with value above $1$, translated in the interior and using uniform regularity, gives a positive interior maximum incompatible with $\Delta v^*=v^{*3}-v^*$.

<a id="eq:L8"></a>
Put $S=\varphi_++\varphi_-$ and $T_*=\varphi_+-\varphi_-$. Adding <a href="#eq:L7" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L7">[eq:L7]</a> gives exactly $$\begin{split}
 L_gS={}&|\nabla h|^2(2g''+S_{tt})
       -2\nabla h\cdot\nabla_y(T_*)_t-\Delta h\,(T_*)_t\\
       &-3g(\varphi_+^2+\varphi_-^2)
                       -(\varphi_+^3+\varphi_-^3).
 \end{split}$$ The terms $\mp\Delta h\,g'$ cancel. Every remaining source is $O(J_0(0))$ in the weighted local $L^{p_0}$ norm with exponent $10\lambda$: the coefficient factors have weight $2\lambda$, the perturbations have weight $3\lambda$, and the cubic terms also have a uniform small bound on one factor. Taking $10\lambda<b_0$ and using the inverse and boundary Sobolev embedding, we conclude $$\label{eq:L8}
 |\partial_t(\varphi_++\varphi_-)(0,0)|\le C J_0(0).$$

Thus the sum of the two artificial boundary slopes differs from the planar value by a curvature-square envelope. We next estimate the change in each slope caused by the actual adjacent sheet, if present.

<a id="eq:L9"></a>
*Step 4: comparing an artificial solution with the true chamber.* We prove, on the upper side with a small adjacent intercept, $$\label{eq:L9}
 |\partial_n(v-v^*_+)(0)|
       \le C\bigl(e^{-\sqrt2\ell}+e^{-cP_0}\bigr).$$ If no small intercept is present, the right side is $Ce^{-cP_0}$. The lower-side assertion compares $-v$ with $v^*_-$. All constants $c>0$ below are fixed before choosing $A_0$.

<a id="eq:L10"></a>
*Middle-chamber deficit.* First we establish a sharp bound at the middle of a chamber. Set $t_1=x_n$ and $t_2=\ell-n'\cdot x$. In the central lateral box, near $t_1=t_2$, both $1-v$ and $1-v^*_+$ satisfy $$\label{eq:L10}
 1-v,\ 1-v^*_+
       \le C\bigl(e^{-\sqrt2 t_1}+e^{-\sqrt2 t_2}+e^{-cP_0}\bigr).$$ Here and below $v$ is positive in the chamber. For the proof, subtract the uniform $o(1)$ graph errors from $t_1$ and $t_2$, and add the lateral face distances of a wider box. Call all these inward unit affine distances $l_1,\ldots,l_k$. Their positive intersection is contained in the phase, by ordering. For either deficit $u=1-v$ or $u=1-v^*_+$, $$(-\Delta+2)u=3u^2-u^3.$$ Sign-ball clearing, as proved with <a href="#eq:phase-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:phase-decay">[eq:phase-decay]</a>, makes $u$ uniformly as small as desired where $\min_jl_j\ge M$, for large fixed $M$. Put $a_0=\sqrt2$ and choose positive constants $C_j$ with $C_je^{-a_0M}=\eta_0$, where $\eta_0>0$ is fixed small. On this inner polytope the function $$\mathcal W=\sum_{j=1}^k
           \left(C_je^{-a_0l_j}-kC_j^2e^{-2a_0l_j}\right)$$ is positive and at most $k\eta_0$. With $r_j=C_je^{-a_0l_j}$, $$(-\Delta+2)\mathcal W=6k\sum_jr_j^2
       \ge6\Bigl(\sum_jr_j\Bigr)^2\ge3\mathcal W^2.$$ Choose $\eta_0$ so $k\eta_0<1/8$, then $M$ so clearing gives $u\le\eta_0/2$ on the boundary. On a boundary face $\mathcal W\ge\eta_0-k\eta_0^2\ge\eta_0/2$. The difference equation has positive zeroth coefficient while $u,\mathcal W$ are small, so the maximum principle gives $u\le\mathcal W$. The central point is a distance comparable to $P_0$ from the lateral faces; the $o(1)$ shifts only change constants. This proves <a href="#eq:L10" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L10">[eq:L10]</a>.

<a id="eq:interface-potential-comparison"></a>
*Potential comparison on the lower half of the chamber.* Now use the domain between $x_n=h(y)$ and the affine midpoint $t_1=t_2$, with $|y|_\infty<c_2P_0$. In the case without a small intercept, use instead its horizontal top at height $c_2P_0$. The difference $u_*=v-v^*_+$ solves $$(-\Delta+V_*)u_*=0,
 \qquad V_*=v^2+vv^*_++(v^*_+)^2-1.$$ For some fixed $\gamma_1>0$, $$\label{eq:interface-potential-comparison}
 V_*(x)\ge V_g(t_1)-\eta(B)e^{-\gamma_1t_1},\qquad
 \eta(B)\longrightarrow0.$$ We check both the fixed-depth and increasing-depth content of this statement. At each base point in the lateral box there is a tangent ball in the positive phase whose radius tends uniformly to infinity. It may be chosen much smaller than $c_1P_0$, the adjacent height, and $B^{-1/4}$: the base Hessian bound keeps the ball above the base, and the chamber and lateral buffers exclude any other zero from it. For a concrete uniform choice, put $H_B=\ell$ when the small adjacent intercept is present and $H_B=c_1P_0$ otherwise, and take $$r_B=\bigl[\min\{c_1P_0,H_B,B^{-1/4}\}\bigr]^{1/2}.$$ All three quantities in the minimum tend to infinity, so $r_B$ is smaller than each by a factor tending to zero. The adjacent height over the larger box is bounded below by a fixed positive fraction of $H_B$, and every base in the smaller box has a lateral margin comparable to $c_1P_0$. Finally $r_B B^{1/4}\to0$, so the Hessian bound excludes a second contact of the tangent ball with the base. The radius therefore works uniformly, including at bases on a lateral face of the smaller box. Lemma <a href="#lem:reach-phase-ball" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:reach-phase-ball">25</a>, or its explicit inequality <a href="#eq:C6" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:C6">[eq:C6]</a>, gives $v\to g(t_1)$ on every fixed depth interval. Indeed, along the inward normal at a base point, that inequality and $|\nabla U|\le1$ give $s-C(1+s)/r\le U\le s$ for fixed $s\ge0$, where $r$ is the radius of the tangent phase ball. These radii tend uniformly to infinity. To pass from normal depth to the vertical coordinate $t_1$, use the uniformly vanishing slope and height of the base graph and the same Lipschitz bound for $U$. Thus the convergence is uniform over all bases in the buffered comparison box; no two-sided estimate for $H$ is used. The artificial solution has the same convergence by construction, and $h=o(1)$ on this box. At increasing depths up to the midpoint there is an explicit phase-ball radius independent of $\theta$. Let $\eta_B=o(1)$ bound the signed affine errors of both sheets in the larger box $|y|_\infty\le10c_1P_0$. For a point in the closure of the smaller lateral comparison box, put $s=t_1\ge1$. We have $t_2\ge s$ and $s\le\theta P_0/4$; the latter follows from $\ell\le\theta P_0/10$ and <a href="#eq:interface-gap-angle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interface-gap-angle">[eq:interface-gap-angle]</a>. Set $c_{\rm ball}=\min(1/8,c'_0)$. On $B_{c_{\rm ball}s}(x)$ both unit affine distances are at least $7s/8>\eta_B$ for small $B$. Its horizontal displacement is at most $c'_0\theta P_0/4=c_1P_0/4$, so it remains inside the larger box even when its center is on a lateral face of the smaller box. It therefore lies in the true positive chamber. It also lies above the artificial graph. Without a small intercept, $s\le c_2P_0$, and the same ball stays below the empty-chamber height $10c_1P_0$. Sign-ball clearing consequently gives $1-v+1-v^*_+\le Ce^{-c_3t_1}$ with $c_3>0$ depending only on the fixed $c_{\rm ball}$ and dimension, before $\theta$ is chosen. Take $0<\gamma_1<\min(c_3,\sqrt2)$. Splitting at a fixed large depth and then using fixed-depth convergence proves <a href="#eq:interface-potential-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interface-potential-comparison">[eq:interface-potential-comparison]</a>. This reasoning includes the domain with the artificial upper face.

<a id="eq:interface-shifted-barrier"></a>
*The barrier at rate $\sqrt2$.* We have bounded the error in the potential by a decaying function with coefficient $\eta(B)\to0$. We now absorb that error into the comparison operator. This is the step that preserves the exact rate. Choose a fixed small $\theta_0>0$; all $t_1$ in the domain are at least $-\theta_0$ for small $B$. Put $a_0=\sqrt2$ and $$Y(t)=g'(t)\left[1+\int_{-\theta_0}^t g'(s)^{-2}\,\,\mathrm ds\right],
 \qquad
 k'(t)=-Y(t)^{-2}\int_{-\theta_0}^tY(s)^2e^{-\gamma_1s}\,\,\mathrm ds.$$ Then $Y>0$, $(-\partial_t^2+V_g)Y=0$, and $Y(t)\asymp e^{a_0t}$ as $t\to\infty$. Consequently $|k'(t)|\le Ce^{-\gamma_1t}$ and its integral is finite. Choose $k(-\theta_0)$ so $1\le k\le C$, and set $\phi=e^{-a_0t}Yk$. Direct differentiation gives $$\label{eq:interface-shifted-barrier}
 \begin{gathered}
 0<c\le\phi\le C,\qquad |\phi'|\le Ce^{-\gamma_1t},\\
 -\phi''-2a_0\phi'+(V_g-2)\phi
       =e^{-a_0t}Y(t)e^{-\gamma_1t}
       \ge c e^{-\gamma_1t}.
 \end{gathered}$$ These bounds also hold on the fixed compact initial interval. If $c'=n'\cdot e_n$, then $$(-\Delta+V_*)\bigl[e^{-a_0t_2}\phi(t_1)\bigr]
 =e^{-a_0t_2}
   \bigl[-\phi''-2a_0c'\phi'+(V_*-2)\phi\bigr].$$ By <a href="#eq:interface-shifted-barrier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interface-shifted-barrier">[eq:interface-shifted-barrier]</a> the bracket is nonnegative when $|1-c'|$ is a sufficiently small fixed number and $B$ is sufficiently small. Its errors are bounded by $C(|1-c'|+\eta(B))e^{-\gamma_1t_1}$. This is the reason the exact exponential $e^{-\sqrt2t_2}$ survives. The small geometric and potential errors have been absorbed in the operator inequality before comparison. They are not additive errors in the interaction estimate and are never divided by its exponentially small scale.

There are also coarse barriers for the remaining faces. Take $v_0$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:reach-half-line-barrier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:reach-half-line-barrier">23</a> with initial endpoint $-\theta_0$. It is bounded above and below by positive constants and satisfies $(-\partial_t^2+V_g)v_0=1$. For any inward affine unit face distance $l$, the function $v_0(t_1)e^{-c_4l}$ is a supersolution of $-\Delta+V_*$ when $c_4>0$ is sufficiently small and $B$ is sufficiently small. Indeed the new terms are bounded by $C(c_4+c_4^2+\eta(B))$, whereas the undisturbed positive term is $1$.

The difference is zero at the bottom. Multiples of these coarse barriers dominate its absolute value at the lateral faces. At the midpoint, <a href="#eq:L10" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L10">[eq:L10]</a> and $t_1=t_2$ bound it by a multiple of $e^{-a_0t_2}\phi(t_1)$ plus $e^{-cP_0}v_0(t_1)$. The latter is itself a supersolution after multiplication by the constant $e^{-cP_0}$. In the case with an artificial top face use its coarse barrier instead. Comparison is legitimate despite the possible sign of $V_*$: division by $v_0$ gives a strictly positive zeroth coefficient, by <a href="#eq:interface-potential-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interface-potential-comparison">[eq:interface-potential-comparison]</a>. The resulting bound on a fixed neighborhood of $0$ is $C(e^{-a_0\ell}+e^{-cP_0})$, or $Ce^{-cP_0}$ without an adjacent small intercept. Boundary gradient estimates for the homogeneous equation on the uniformly smooth graph, with zero boundary value, prove <a href="#eq:L9" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L9">[eq:L9]</a>.

*Step 5: choosing the constants and recovering the base defect.* The artificial cancellation and the true-chamber comparison are now proved separately. To combine them uniformly, use the following order for the constants. First fix the decay and comparison constants for $g$, the half-space inverse, and the elementary phase-ball clearing. The box construction above has uniformly controlled aspect ratios when both fractions are proportional to $\theta$, so choose $\gamma_1$ and then $\phi,v_0$ using those uniform constants. Next take $\theta$ small enough for the plateau condition and the drift error in <a href="#eq:interface-shifted-barrier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interface-shifted-barrier">[eq:interface-shifted-barrier]</a>; choose $c_1,c_2$ as specified. The resulting coarse exponent $c>0$ is fixed. Now choose $A_0$ large enough for the realizing-contact argument and $cA_0>1$. Then choose $\lambda$ small enough for $10\lambda A_0<1/2$ and $10\lambda<b_0$. Finally decrease the upper threshold for $B$ to meet all small-error conditions. This order is independent of $\delta$. In particular $e^{-cP_0}\le B$ with uniform constants.

At $0$, the two unsigned true derivatives in the inward height coordinates are both $\partial_nv(0)$. Equations <a href="#eq:L8" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L8">[eq:L8]</a> and <a href="#eq:L9" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L9">[eq:L9]</a> show $$|2\partial_nv(0)-2g'(0)|
       \le C\bigl(J_0(0)+E(0)+e^{-cP_0}\bigr)\le CB,$$ because every adjacent gap used satisfies $d(0)\le G_1\ell$. Since $D(0)=1-2\partial_nv(0)^2$ and $g'(0)=1/\sqrt2$, this proves the first inequality of <a href="#eq:L5" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L5">[eq:L5]</a> and completes the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-49"></a>
###### An integral comparison

The pointwise comparison contains the supremum $J_0$. For the growth argument we need an integral estimate involving $a^4$, $D^2$ and $E^2$ instead. Uniform local area and derivative bounds let us replace the supremum by a positive convolution, with a small $D^2$ error that can be absorbed. The constants must remain independent of the eventual choice of the defect threshold and of the center of the spatial weight.

<a id="stmt-lem-interface-good-area"></a>
<div id="lem:interface-good-area" class="lemma">

**Lemma 34** (Uniform area on good pieces). *For $0<\delta\le1/4$, the area of $\Sigma\setminus X$ in any unit ball is bounded by a dimensional constant, independently of $\delta$. Consequently its area in an ambient ball of radius $r\ge1$ is at most $C(1+r)^n$. At a good zero whose distance from $X$ exceeds $2$, there is a fixed-size graph neighborhood on which $D$ is comparable to its value at that zero and $|\nabla_\Sigma A|\le C\sqrt D$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-interface-good-area"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-interface-good-area</summary>

*Proof.* At a good zero, $|\nabla v|\ge\sqrt{3/8}$. Uniform second derivative bounds for $v$ give a fixed ball on which its derivative along the initial normal is bounded below. All zeros in a smaller ball therefore form one graph, with uniformly bounded slope and derivatives. A separated selection of such smaller balls has bounded cardinality inside a unit ball, by ordinary ambient packing, and covers its good zeros. Summing their graph areas gives the claim. Larger balls are covered by $C(1+r)^n$ unit balls. The last assertions are local Harnack and the differentiated bounds in Lemma <a href="#lem:diffuse-local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:diffuse-local">8</a>, applied on a fixed regular graph contained in the good set. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-interface-integral"></a>
<div id="lem:interface-integral" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:L11"></a>
**Lemma 35** (Weighted integral comparison). *Let $\mathcal X$ be a maximal unit-separated subset of $X$, with $\mathcal X=\varnothing$ when $X=\varnothing$. For an arbitrary center $x_c$, set $\chi_R(x)=\exp(-\sqrt{1+|x-x_c|^2}/R)$. There are $C_D,R_D<\infty$, independent of $0<\delta\le1/4$ and $x_c$, such that for $R\ge R_D$, $$\label{eq:L11}
 \int_{\Sigma\setminus X}(D^2+E^2)\chi_R^2\,\,\mathrm dS
 \le C_D\left\{
 \int_{\Sigma\setminus X}(a^4+DE)\chi_R^2\,\,\mathrm dS
             +\sum_{x\in\mathcal X}\chi_R(x)^2\right\}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-interface-integral"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-interface-integral</summary>

*Proof.* Multiplying the first inequality of <a href="#eq:L5" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L5">[eq:L5]</a> by $D$ and the second by $E$, and absorbing $DJ_0,EJ_0$ by Young’s inequality, gives $D^2+E^2\le C(J_0^2+DE)$. We estimate $J_0^2$ without replacing a supremum by an unjustified point value integral. Let $w$ be good and $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(w,X)>2$. On its fixed regular graph, for $w'$ within intrinsic distance $\mu$, Lemma <a href="#lem:interface-good-area" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:interface-good-area">34</a> gives $$a(w)^4\le C a(w')^4+C\mu^4D(w)^2.$$ Average on that small graph disk, and use Harnack and a fixed-size graph disk to bound $D(w)^2$ by the integral of $D^2$ in $B_1(w)$. For every sufficiently small fixed $\mu>0$ this yields $$a(w)^4\le C_\mu\int_{B_1(w)\cap(\Sigma\setminus X)}a^4\,\,\mathrm dS
       +C\mu^4\int_{B_1(w)\cap(\Sigma\setminus X)}D^2\,\,\mathrm dS.$$ The coefficient in front of the last integral is independent of $\mu$. Values at $w$ within distance $2$ of $X$, including bad points where $\bar a=1$, are bounded by a constant and can instead be charged to some point of $\mathcal X$ within distance $3$. Since bounded shifts change $e^{-2\lambda|z-w|}$ by only a fixed factor, taking the supremum over $w$ gives $$J_0(z)^2\le C\int e^{-2\lambda|z-w|}
   \left(C_\mu a^4\,\,\mathrm dS+C\mu^4D^2\,\,\mathrm dS
                                  +\,\mathrm d\#_{\mathcal X}\right)(w).$$ The surface measures here are restricted to $\Sigma\setminus X$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:interface-good-area" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:interface-good-area">34</a> and $\chi_R(z)\le e^{|z-w|/R}\chi_R(w)$, for $R\ge2/\lambda$, $$\int_{\Sigma\setminus X}
   e^{-2\lambda|z-w|}\chi_R(z)^2\,\,\mathrm dS_z
       \le C_\lambda\chi_R(w)^2.$$ The constant is independent of the good threshold, by summation over unit annuli and their polynomial area bound. Tonelli now bounds the integral of $J_0^2\chi_R^2$. Choose $\mu$ small enough to absorb the resulting $D^2$ term, after the constant in <a href="#eq:L5" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L5">[eq:L5]</a> and $\lambda$ have been fixed. This proves <a href="#eq:L11" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L11">[eq:L11]</a>. All integrals are finite: $D,E,a$ are bounded on good zeros, the area has polynomial growth, and the separated net has polynomial ambient growth. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-50"></a>
###### Sharp constants in logarithmic windows

The comparison above fixes the exponential rate, with unspecified multiplicative constants. On windows whose defect decreases at a polynomial rate, the sheets are sufficiently close to parallel planes to compute the coefficients as well. We first make the geometry and the additive distance errors precise; these errors must tend to zero before they are inserted into an exponential.

<a id="stmt-lem-interface-log-windows"></a>
<div id="lem:interface-log-windows" class="lemma">

**Lemma 36** (Uniform geometry on clean windows). *Fix $\omega>0$, $C_0<\infty$, and a fractional margin $0<\theta_0<1$. Suppose that $z_0\in\Sigma$ and $$D(w)\le N^{-\omega}\qquad
 (w\in\Sigma\cap B_{N^\omega}(z_0)),\qquad N\longrightarrow\infty.$$ The following conclusions hold uniformly for bases $z\in\Sigma\cap B_{(1-\theta_0)N^\omega}(z_0)$. Local zero pieces within $C_0\log N$ of $z$ are finitely many ordered smooth graphs over $T_z\Sigma$. Their domains contain a disk of radius $cN^{\omega/8}$, with a further comparable graph buffer. They have curvature at most $CN^{-\omega/2}$ and slopes and differences of unoriented normals at most $$C\bigl(N^{-\omega/8}\log N+N^{-3\omega/8}\bigr).$$ Distinct graphs have Euclidean separation at least $c_0\log N$ where they participate in these neighborhoods.*

*For every fixed $k>0$, these descriptions extend over lateral disks of radius $(\log N)^k$ and through every fixed multiple of $\log N$ in normal depth. They contain every zero piece meeting the corresponding smaller cylinder. Projection to each participating local graph is unique and smooth there. Differences between its Euclidean distance and its vertical distance, and differences between its height increments and those of its tangent plane, are additive errors bounded by $$C_kN^{-c}(1+\log N)^{C_k}=o(1),\qquad c=c(\omega)>0.$$ The same bounds hold uniformly as the center moves in the stated fractional interior. In particular the lateral domains include $(\log N)^2$ boxes and disks of radius $\ell^{2/3}$ whenever $\ell=O(\log N)$. The polynomial buffers and unique projections make these descriptions consistent on overlaps.*

*If a distinct graph is within $C_0\log N$ of the base $z$, then $$d(z)=G_1\ell_{\min}(z),$$ where $\ell_{\min}(z)$ is the shortest Euclidean distance from $z$ to an adjacent graph. All thresholds may depend on the fixed parameters and on $k$, but not on the solution or the base.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-interface-log-windows"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-interface-log-windows</summary>

*Proof.* We establish graph continuation on a polynomial scale before using it on any logarithmic scale. Put $$\kappa_N=CN^{-\omega/2},\qquad r=N^{\omega/8}.$$ At every zero under consideration, $|A|\le\kappa_N$ and $|\nabla v|$ has a fixed positive lower bound. The fractional margin contains all balls of radius $100r$ used below once $N$ is large. Starting at any such zero, lift a straight radial segment in its tangent plane by tangent projection. As long as the lift has length at most $10r$, its normal changes by at most $10\kappa_Nr=o(1)$. The differential of projection therefore remains invertible, and the lifted length is at most twice the projected length. In particular every segment of projected length at most $r$ continues to its endpoint: a terminating sequence stays in the larger ball, has a regular zero as its limit, and local inversion continues it. The lifting equation depends smoothly on the segment endpoint. Its endpoints thus form one smooth graph over the full tangent disk; local uniqueness identifies it with the original zero piece. Starting with a larger fixed multiple of $r$ supplies the stated buffer. Taylor’s formula on these graphs gives the affine error $$C\kappa_Nr^2=O(N^{-\omega/4}).$$

<a id="eq:interface-clean-angle"></a>
We next put nearby pieces over the same plane. Two such graph disks whose base points are at distance $b=o(r)$ have unoriented normal angle at most $$\label{eq:interface-clean-angle}
 C\left(\frac b r+\kappa_Nr\right).$$ Indeed, if the angle were larger, a line in one tangent plane in the direction of maximal tilt would make its signed distance from the other tangent plane take opposite signs, with magnitude greater than both affine errors, inside a fixed smaller disk. Points of that graph project into the interior of the other disk. The signed graph height, measured in the second disk’s normal direction, then vanishes by the intermediate value theorem. At this intersection the two normals would still differ by more than their variations $C\kappa_Nr$, contradicting the unique tangent plane of the regular level set. This argument also excludes differently tilted pieces of a single connected component. Taking $b=O(\log N)$ proves the claimed common-plane slope bound. Over a common smaller disk the graphs either agree or are disjoint, and so are ordered.

For completeness, no new sheet can enter unnoticed through the side of a logarithmic-power box. A zero $w$ with lateral coordinate $|y|\le(\log N)^k$ and height at most $C_0\log N$ first has $b=O((1+\log N)^{\max(k,1)})$ in <a href="#eq:interface-clean-angle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interface-clean-angle">[eq:interface-clean-angle]</a>. This already gives a slope tending to zero polynomially and permits its graph to continue to the normal axis through $z$. Its height changes on that continuation by at most $$C\left(\frac{(1+\log N)^{\max(k,1)}}r+\kappa_Nr\right)
       (1+\log N)^k=o(1).$$ Its intercept is therefore within $(C_0+1)\log N$ of $z$ for large $N$. Applying <a href="#eq:interface-clean-angle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interface-clean-angle">[eq:interface-clean-angle]</a> again at this intercept gives the refined slope bound already stated. Enlarging the fixed depth constant in a buffered description therefore includes all pieces in the smaller box. There are only finitely many in each closed inner cylinder: otherwise zeros on distinct graphs accumulate at a regular zero, whose uniform local graph neighborhood contains only one zero piece. This is local finiteness, and imposes no global bound on the number of sheets.

The same estimates give the required additive accuracy. On a disk of radius $L=(\log N)^k$, the error from a graph’s own tangent plane is at most $C\kappa_NL^2$. If $\vartheta_N=C(\log N/r+\kappa_Nr)$ denotes the common-plane slope bound, changing from its tangent plane to the common horizontal plane costs at most $C\vartheta_NL+C\kappa_NL^2$ in height. Both quantities are bounded by a negative power of $N$ times a fixed logarithmic power. They tend to zero as numbers, rather than only relative to the gap.

To check projections, write one graph as $x_n=f(y)$. For a fixed point $x$, half its squared distance to $(y,f(y))$ has Hessian $$\mathrm{Id}+\nabla f\otimes\nabla f-(x_n-f)D^2f.$$ On a buffered disk relevant to logarithmic depth this is positive definite, with eigenvalues $1+o(1)$: the slope tends to zero and $|x_n-f||D^2f|=o(1)$. The same bound holds out to a fixed fraction of the polynomial disk, since its height variation is at most $C\vartheta_Nr+C\kappa_Nr^2=O(\log N)$. A minimizing point cannot occur at that outer boundary, whose horizontal distance is polynomially large, whereas the vertical competitor has distance $O(\log N)$. Strict convexity gives a unique foot, and the implicit function theorem gives its smooth dependence on $x$. The stationary equation bounds the horizontal displacement of the foot by $C\vartheta_N\log N$ and the difference between true and vertical distances by $C\vartheta_N^2\log N$, after harmless enlargement of the common slope bound. These are the asserted additive errors. This projection is to a specified local graph, not to an entire connected component. Every foot that can compete for the nearest zero lies within logarithmic distance, and hence belongs to the buffered family by the preceding completeness argument.

To prove separation, set $r_0=c_0\log N$ with $c_0>0$ sufficiently small. Starting at any participating zero, the exponential bound in <a href="#eq:diffuse-local" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:diffuse-local">[eq:diffuse-local]</a> gives $D\le CN^{-\omega/2}$ throughout $B_{4r_0}$. Consequently $|T|\le CN^{-\omega/4}$ there. Integration shows that $p$ remains uniformly close to the initial unit normal, so its component in that direction is positive. All zeros in a smaller cylinder then form a single graph. In conjunction with the common graph description, this excludes a distinct sheet at distance less than a smaller fixed multiple of $r_0$.

Finally assume a distinct sheet lies within $C_0\log N$ of $z$. Its nearest foot is interior to the polynomial graph disk, and the shortest chord is normal to that graph. The common normal estimate puts its angle at $z$ in the plateau of $\Gamma$, for large $N$. It supplies a penalized competitor of size $O(\log N)$. Every better competitor has chord length $O(\log N)$ because $\Gamma\ge1$, so lies in the same graph system. A chord of length $l$ to the base graph has normal displacement at most $C\kappa_N l^2$. Since $\Gamma(s)\ge1/s$, its penalized length is at least $c\kappa_N^{-1}\gg\log N$, also in a diagonal limit. For every other graph all chords cost at least $G_1$ times their length, and its shortest chord attains this factor. Only adjacent graphs can minimize, since a chord to a farther one crosses an intervening zero first. This proves the exact identity for $d$. All graph identifications and projections on overlapping inner windows agree by their local uniqueness; all construction scales fit within the original fractional margin. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-interface-sharp-gaps"></a>
<div id="prop:interface-sharp-gaps" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:L12"></a>
<a id="eq:L13"></a>
**Proposition 37** (Sharp interaction and middle-phase tail). *Under the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#lem:interface-log-windows" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:interface-log-windows">36</a>, for each $\varepsilon>0$ there is $P_\varepsilon<\infty$ such that every fixed $P\ge P_\varepsilon$ has the following properties when $N$ is sufficiently large. If a different sheet is within $C_0\log N$ of the base, then $$\label{eq:L12}
 I_P(z)\ge(32-\varepsilon)e^{-\sqrt2\ell_{\min}(z)}.$$ If $x$ lies between two adjacent sheets, their distances $s_i,s_j$ from $x$ are both at most $C_0\log N$, they are the nearest sheets, and $|s_i-s_j|\le C_1$ for a fixed $C_1$, then $$\label{eq:L13}
 q(x)\le(4+\varepsilon)
       \left(e^{-\sqrt2s_i}+e^{-\sqrt2s_j}\right).$$ Largeness of $N$ may depend on all the fixed parameters, including $P,C_1,\varepsilon$. The window geometry and conclusions are uniform in the solution and center.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-interface-sharp-gaps"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-interface-sharp-gaps</summary>

*Proof.* For <a href="#eq:L12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L12">[eq:L12]</a> let $\ell=\ell_{\min}$ and fix $n_0=\nu(z)$ in <a href="#eq:L6" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L6">[eq:L6]</a>. On the column $z+s\nu(z)$, $|s|\le P$, <a href="#eq:interface-normal-accuracy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interface-normal-accuracy">[eq:interface-normal-accuracy]</a> and local Harnack imply $$D=2(1-p\cdot n_0)-|p-n_0|^2,
 \qquad qD\ge(2-o(1))w.$$ In fact $|p-n_0|^2=O_P(D(z)^2)$ whereas $D$ is comparable to $D(z)$ there, so the last error is uniform and relative. At the opposite foot use a tangent disk of radius $\ell^{2/3}$ and bounded depth $|t|\le Q$ in its affine normal coordinates. The true orientations are opposed, and all profile and affine errors on this patch tend uniformly to zero by Lemma <a href="#lem:interface-log-windows" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:interface-log-windows">36</a> and <a href="#eq:interface-normal-accuracy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interface-normal-accuracy">[eq:interface-normal-accuracy]</a> at its bases. Thus the exact source on the right of <a href="#eq:L6" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L6">[eq:L6]</a> satisfies $$3qw-q^2D\ge(6-o(1))G(t)^2,\qquad |t|\le Q.$$ The constant is $6$, since $w\to2G$ there. For fixed $P,Q$ the comparison is relative, as $G$ has a positive minimum on the compact depth interval.

Integrating the heat-kernel formula for $\mathcal K$ in its horizontal variables gives exactly $$\int_{\mathbb R^m}\mathcal K(y,h)\,\,\mathrm dy
       =\frac1{2\sqrt2}e^{-\sqrt2|h|}.$$ Truncating this integral to $|y|\le\ell^{2/3}$ changes it by a relative $o(1)$ for $h=\ell+O(P+Q)$: the excess distance outside that disk is at least $c\ell^{1/3}$, and the kernel bounds used above control the remaining polynomial volume factor. Polynomial-small graph and angular errors contribute only $o(1)$ additive errors to distances on this disk, so do not change the exponential factor by more than $1+o(1)$. It follows, with signs depending on the side but irrelevant by symmetry of $G$, that $$\begin{split}
 \frac{I_P(z)}{e^{-\sqrt2\ell}}
 &\ge \frac{2-o(1)}\sigma\frac{6-o(1)}{2\sqrt2}
 \left(\int_{-P}^{P}e^{\sqrt2s}G(s)^2\,\,\mathrm ds\right)
 \left(\int_{-Q}^{Q}e^{\sqrt2t}G(t)^2\,\,\mathrm dt\right).
 \end{split}$$ Choose $P,Q$ large and fixed, then $N$ large. The elementary substitution $r=e^{\sqrt2t}$ gives $$\int_\mathbb Re^{\sqrt2t}G(t)^2\,\,\mathrm dt=2\sigma.$$ The limiting coefficient is therefore $$\frac2\sigma\frac6{2\sqrt2}(2\sigma)^2=32,$$ which proves <a href="#eq:L12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L12">[eq:L12]</a>.

The complementary estimate concerns the value of the phase between the same sheets. Here the coefficient comes from the one-dimensional tail $1-g(s)\sim2e^{-\sqrt2s}$ and from $q\le2(1-v)$ in a positive phase. The lateral boundary must be placed farther away than the logarithmic sheet separation so that its contribution is negligible relative to the principal tails.

For <a href="#eq:L13" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L13">[eq:L13]</a>, change the sign of $v$ if necessary so the phase between the sheets is positive. Separation and $|s_i-s_j|\le C_1$ imply $\min(s_i,s_j)\to\infty$, so the point $x$ belongs to the fixed-depth inner polytope used below once $N$ is sufficiently large. Use the two tangent planes at the perpendicular feet from $x$, together with lateral faces at scale $(\log N)^2$. Lemma <a href="#lem:interface-log-windows" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:interface-log-windows">36</a> provides these graphs with polynomial buffers, no intervening zeros, and affine errors $o(1)$. Subtract these errors from the two face distances, producing an inner polytope as in the proof of <a href="#eq:L10" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L10">[eq:L10]</a>. Its distances at $x$ are $s_i+o(1)$ and $s_j+o(1)$.

In that proof the two principal coefficients $C_j$ can now be chosen equal to $2+\eta$, with arbitrarily small fixed $\eta>0$. Indeed at every point of their inner boundaries $l_j=M$, including intersections with lateral faces, the true perpendicular projection is unique and remains in the polynomially buffered clean window. The signed distance to its sheet is $M+o(1)$: the affine error on the entire $(\log N)^2$ box is polynomial-small, including its gradient, and the squared-distance Hessian in Lemma <a href="#lem:interface-log-windows" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:interface-log-windows">36</a> is positive definite. Fixed-depth profile convergence at these feet is uniform, so the solution converges there to $g(M+o(1))$. Since $1-g(M)=2e^{-\sqrt2M}+O(e^{-2\sqrt2M})$, choose $M$ large so that $$(2+\eta)e^{-\sqrt2M}
       -k(2+\eta)^2e^{-2\sqrt2M}>1-g(M)$$ with fixed positive slack, and then $N$ large enough to retain it. Keep the coarse fixed coefficients on the lateral faces. Each summand in the barrier is nonnegative; thus intersections of faces cause no difficulty in the boundary comparison. The same algebra $(-\Delta+2)\mathcal W\ge3\mathcal W^2$ applies with these unequal coefficients. At $x$, the lateral contribution is $O(e^{-c(\log N)^2})$, hence negligible relative to either of the two exponentials, since $s_i,s_j\le C_0\log N$. We obtain $$1-v(x)\le(2+2\eta)
       \left(e^{-\sqrt2s_i}+e^{-\sqrt2s_j}\right).$$ Finally $q=(1-v)(1+v)\le2(1-v)$; take $\eta$ sufficiently small in terms of $\varepsilon$ to prove <a href="#eq:L13" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L13">[eq:L13]</a>. ◻

</details>

The two sharp coefficients concern different quantities: $32$ bounds normal defect mass from below, while $4$ bounds the phase tail from above. The radial test will compare them on the same adjacent gap. The fourth-order estimate proved next first supplies the control of rough regions needed to make that comparison globally.

<a id="pm-h-51"></a>
##### A fourth-order growth estimate

The reach and interface estimates are local. We now integrate them to bound the portions of the zero set on which the radial argument will incur errors: large defect, appreciable curvature, or short reach. Throughout this section $v:\mathbb R^7\to(-1,1)$ is an entire stable solution of $\Delta v=v^3-v$, $D>0$, and its energy density is unbounded, as in Sections <a href="#sec:reach" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reach">1</a> and <a href="#sec:interface" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:interface">2</a>. In particular $m=6$. Recall that $a=|A|$, $d$ is the penalized reach, and $G_1$ is the constant value of its angular penalty near normal incidence. Write $$K(R)=1+\sup_{x\in\mathbb R^7}M_R(x),\qquad
 \kappa=\frac{\sqrt2}{G_1},\qquad E=e^{-\kappa d}.$$ The target scale is $K(R)R^{m-4}$ for the fourth curvature moment and the squared defect and interaction. Its gain of four powers over the weighted area scale $K(R)R^m$ will pay for rough regions and curvature errors in Section <a href="#sec:radial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:radial">1</a>, even when $K(R)$ grows arbitrarily slowly. There are three steps. Simons’ identity reduces the curvature moment to a small multiple of the squared defect. A single ambient stability test then supplies both surface mass and mass near bad zeros. Finally, a multiplier for the reach inequality bounds the derivative cost of that test. The integral comparison of Lemma <a href="#lem:interface-integral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:interface-integral">35</a> will close the three estimates.

All surface integrals in this section use induced area. Fix a threshold $\delta\in(0,1/4)$ provisionally and define $$X=\{z\in\Sigma:D(z)\ge\delta\},\qquad
 \Sigma_g=\Sigma\setminus X,$$ and let $\mathcal X\subset X$ be any maximal unit-separated set. Put $$\chi(x)=\chi_{R,x_c}(x)
 =\exp\!\left(-\frac{\sqrt{1+|x-x_c|^2}}R\right),\qquad
 N_R=\sum_{x\in\mathcal X}\chi(x)^2,
 \qquad S_0=\frac{a^2+I_P}{2}.$$ Here $I_P$ is the normal average defined in Lemma <a href="#lem:interface-sharp-means" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:interface-sharp-means">28</a>; its finite depth $P$ will be fixed below. Thus $S_0$ is the favorable mass in the strengthened surface inequality. The convention when $X=\varnothing$ is $N_R=0$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,X)=+\infty$. Since $D=1$ at a singular zero, every such zero belongs to $X$. The quantity $N_R$ records the bad set through any maximal unit-separated net, rather than requiring that set to be smooth.

<a id="stmt-prop-growth-fourth-order"></a>
<div id="prop:growth-fourth-order" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:M1"></a>
**Proposition 38** (Fourth-order growth). *In the seven-dimensional stable setting above, fix the penalty $\Gamma$ sufficiently close to the choices specified in Section <a href="#sec:reach" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reach">1</a>, with $\eta_0<10^{-4}$ and $G_1-1,(1-\delta_2)^{-1}-1<10^{-3}$, decreasing these errors if required by Section <a href="#sec:radial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:radial">1</a>. There are a finite $P$, a positive $\delta$, and constants $R_0,C<\infty$ such that, for every solution in the setting above, every $x_c\in\mathbb R^7$, every maximal net $\mathcal X$, and every $R\ge R_0$, $$\label{eq:M1}
 \mathcal Y_R:=
 \int_{\Sigma_g}\chi^2(a^4+D^2+E^2)+N_R
 \le C K(R)R^{m-4}.$$ The constants depend on the fixed penalty and tolerances, including $\delta$, but are independent of the solution, the center, the net, and $R$. Any prescribed smaller positive tolerances in <a href="#eq:L1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L1">[eq:L1]</a>–<a href="#eq:L4" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L4">[eq:L4]</a> and <a href="#eq:L12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L12">[eq:L12]</a>–<a href="#eq:L13" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L13">[eq:L13]</a> may be imposed before $P$ and $\delta$ are fixed.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-52"></a>
###### Order of choices.

<a id="eq:growth-sharp-budget"></a>
The threshold $\delta$ must remain fixed while the spatial radius grows; otherwise the bad set and its net could change with $R$. First fix the penalty and the threshold-independent constant $C_D$ in <a href="#eq:L11" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L11">[eq:L11]</a>. Choose the small coefficient in the curvature estimate below in terms of $C_D$. Next fix the interface tolerances, including $\tau\le .001$ for Lemma <a href="#lem:growth-gluing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:growth-gluing">40</a>, the normal depth $P$, and the required lower reach threshold $t_{\min}$. Only then shrink $\delta$ so that the interface estimates hold on $\Sigma_g$ and $d\ge t_{\min}$ there. In particular, throughout the remaining proof, $$\label{eq:growth-sharp-budget}
 I:=I_P\ge .999a^2,\qquad c_I D\le I\le C_P D,
 \qquad |H|\le\left(\frac1{2\sqrt2}+\varepsilon_H\right)I,
 \quad \varepsilon_H\le .001.$$ The value of $\varepsilon_H$ can also be small enough that $|H|\le .355I$. After $\delta$ is fixed, choose a collar width $L_0$ about $X$. The reach is bounded on that collar, so a compact interval $[t_{\min},t_*]$ can cover all its reach values. The later test amplitude $f(d)$ will equal one on this interval. Its decay and the parameters that join its different decay ranges are chosen last. All these choices precede every spatial exhaustion and the limit $R\to\infty$.

<a id="pm-h-53"></a>
###### Area and collar bounds.

<a id="eq:growth-area"></a>
<a id="eq:growth-collar-count"></a>
Two elementary estimates will justify all weighted integrations: $$\label{eq:growth-area}
 K(t)\le C\frac tR K(R)\quad(t\ge R\ge1),\qquad
 \int_{\Sigma_g}\chi^2\le CK(R)R^m.$$ To prove the first, cover a ball of radius $t$ by at most $C(t/R)^n$ balls of radius $R$ and divide the resulting energy bound by $t^m$. For the second, the uniformly regular small charts at good zeros bound their area by the energy in fixed enlargements; use a bounded overlap covering and then exponentially weighted annuli. The same charts, or Lemma <a href="#lem:interface-good-area" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:interface-good-area">34</a>, imply $$\label{eq:growth-collar-count}
 \int_{\Sigma_g\cap\{\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(\cdot,X)\le s\}}\chi^2
 +\int_{\{\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(\cdot,X)\le s\}}\chi^2\,\,\mathrm dx
 \le C(1+s)^n e^{C(1+s)/R}N_R\quad(s\ge1).$$ Indeed maximality of the net places $X$ in its unit balls, and each enlarged ball has good area and volume at most $C(1+s)^n$. Shifting the weight through that ball costs at most $e^{C(1+s)/R}$. These arguments also prove finiteness of every integral used below.

<a id="pm-h-54"></a>
###### Simons’ identity and the curvature moment

We first estimate $a^4$ with a coefficient of $D^2$ that can be made arbitrarily small. The method combines Simons’ identity, a strict Kato gain, and stability, following the curvature-estimate arguments of Simons (1968; Schoen, Simon, and Yau 1975) <a id="pm-cite-53"></a>[[Simons1968](#ref-Simons1968), [SchoenSimonYau1975](#ref-SchoenSimonYau1975)]. Our surface need not be minimal. We therefore retain both the derivatives of $H=\operatorname{tr}A$ and the cubic-curvature error involving $H$; the averaged mean-derivative estimate <a href="#eq:L2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L2">[eq:L2]</a> will make their cost sufficiently small.

<a id="stmt-lem-growth-curvature"></a>
<div id="lem:growth-curvature" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:M2"></a>
**Lemma 39** (Curvature controlled by a small defect cost). *For each $\varepsilon_2>0$ there are choices of the interface tolerances and $\delta>0$ such that, uniformly for all centers and sufficiently large $R$, $$\label{eq:M2}
 A_4:=\int_{\Sigma_g}\chi^2a^4
 \le\varepsilon_2\int_{\Sigma_g}\chi^2D^2
       +C_{\varepsilon_2,\delta}N_R
       +C_{\varepsilon_2,\delta}K(R)R^{m-4}.$$ The constants remain valid on decreasing $\delta$ once the preceding interface tolerances have been fixed, except for the displayed allowed dependence of the last two coefficients on $\delta$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-growth-curvature"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-growth-curvature</summary>

*Proof.* The strict gradient gain lets Simons’ identity and surface stability control one another. We derive that gain first, apply it away from $X$, and then charge the omitted collar to $N_R$.

<a id="pm-h-55"></a>
###### The Kato gain.

<a id="eq:growth-kato"></a>
We first record the algebra behind the strict gradient gain. If $A$ is a symmetric tensor and $\nabla A$ is totally symmetric, there are dimensional $\theta>0,C$ such that $$\label{eq:growth-kato}
 |\nabla A|^2\ge(1+\theta)|\nabla|A||^2-C|\nabla H|^2.$$ For $|A|=|\nabla A|=1$, equality in ordinary Kato requires $\nabla_iA=b_iA$. Total symmetry, after placing $b$ in the first coordinate direction, forces $A$ to have only its $11$ component. Consequently $\nabla H=b\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A\ne0$. Compactness of the normalized finite-dimensional tensor sets therefore gives a strict Kato gap when $|\nabla H|$ is sufficiently small; when it is larger, the last term in <a href="#eq:growth-kato" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:growth-kato">[eq:growth-kato]</a> pays for the inequality. Homogeneity proves the claim. The weak gradient of $|A|$ is zero almost everywhere on its zero set, so this includes that set.

<a id="pm-h-56"></a>
###### Simons’ identity away from bad zeros.

<a id="eq:growth-simons"></a>
Choose the averaging radius $S$ in <a href="#eq:L2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L2">[eq:L2]</a>. Let $m_c$ be a Lipschitz cutoff vanishing within distance $3S$ of $X$, equal to one outside distance $6S$, and with bounded gradient; take $m_c=1$ if $X$ is empty. Put $u=\chi m_c$. The hypersurface identity, with our convention $A=\,\mathrm d\nu$, is $$\Delta_\Sigma A=\nabla_\Sigma^2H+HA^2-a^2A.$$ Pair with $A u^2$ and integrate by parts. Codazzi gives $\operatorname{div}_\Sigma A=\nabla_\Sigma H$. Thus the term with $\nabla^2H$ costs at most $C\int u^2|\nabla H|^2+C\int a^2|\nabla u|^2$; the cross from $\Delta A$ uses $\langle A,\nabla_iA\rangle=a\nabla_i a$ and costs an arbitrarily small fraction of $\int u^2|\nabla a|^2$ plus $C\int a^2|\nabla u|^2$. Since $|H|\le CD$, <a href="#eq:growth-kato" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:growth-kato">[eq:growth-kato]</a> yields, for a dimensional $\theta'>0$, $$\label{eq:growth-simons}
 (1+\theta')G\le F+
 C\int u^2\bigl(|\nabla H|^2+Da^3\bigr)
 +C\int a^2|\nabla u|^2,
 \quad G=\int u^2|\nabla a|^2,\quad F=\int u^2a^4.$$ Applying <a href="#eq:L3" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L3">[eq:L3]</a> to $au$, and using the curvature lower bound in <a href="#eq:L1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L1">[eq:L1]</a> with sufficiently small tolerance, gives $$F\le(1+\theta'/2)G+C\int a^2|\nabla u|^2.$$ Subtracting this from <a href="#eq:growth-simons" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:growth-simons">[eq:growth-simons]</a> controls both $G$ and $F$ by the remaining errors.

<a id="pm-h-57"></a>
###### Paying the mean-curvature and collar errors.

It remains to control the errors in <a href="#eq:growth-simons" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:growth-simons">[eq:growth-simons]</a> without losing the small coefficient of $D^2$. For every center in this application there is one uniform sheet on $B_{4S}(z)$. Fubini in <a href="#eq:L2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L2">[eq:L2]</a> therefore gives $$\int u^2|\nabla H|^2
 \le C\varepsilon\int_{\Sigma_g}\chi^2D^2.$$ The averaging balls do not meet $X$. For each fixed point counted by the integral, its centers lie on one chart of area at most $CS^m$; the factor $S^{-m}$ cancels this bound. The ratio of the two weights is at most $e^{2S/R}$, hence at most two for sufficiently large $R$. This explains why the constant multiplying $\varepsilon$ is independent of $S$. Furthermore $a^2\le CD$ implies $Da^3\le C\sqrt\delta D^2$. The cutoff cost is bounded by $$C_S N_R+CR^{-2}\int_{\Sigma_g}\chi^2a^2
 \le C_SN_R+\varepsilon' A_4
      +C_{\varepsilon'}R^{-4}\int_{\Sigma_g}\chi^2.$$ The discarded part of $A_4$, where $m_c\ne1$, is also $C_SN_R$ by <a href="#eq:growth-collar-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:growth-collar-count">[eq:growth-collar-count]</a>. Choose $\varepsilon'$ small enough to absorb, then the tolerance in <a href="#eq:L2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L2">[eq:L2]</a> and $\delta$ small enough for the prescribed $\varepsilon_2$. Equation <a href="#eq:growth-area" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:growth-area">[eq:growth-area]</a> proves <a href="#eq:M2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:M2">[eq:M2]</a>. All integrations may first have compact support; the exponential weight, polynomial area bound, and uniform unit derivative bounds remove the additional cutoffs. Approximation of $a$ by $(a^2+\varepsilon^2)^{1/2}$ justifies its use as a test. ◻

</details>

From now on fix $\varepsilon_2$ sufficiently small relative to $C_D$ in <a href="#eq:L11" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L11">[eq:L11]</a>, and make all the preceding parameter choices. Lower bounds on $t_{\min}$ used below involve only the already fixed reach and interface constants. Lemma <a href="#lem:reach-small-defect" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:reach-small-defect">20</a> permits us to enforce them by the final choice of $\delta$.

<a id="pm-h-58"></a>
###### Gluing ambient and surface stability

The curvature estimate leaves a cost proportional to $N_R$. To control it, stability must detect defect near $X$, where a regular surface test is unavailable. The ambient amplitude $q=1-v^2$ does this. Away from $X$, lifting the normal profile $G(s)=1-g(s)^2$ supplies the stronger surface mass $S_0$. We now combine those amplitudes into one physical test.

Combining ambient and transition-set tests is also part of the free-boundary methods of Chan, Fernández-Real, Figalli, and Serra (2026) <a id="pm-cite-54"></a>[[CFFS2025](#ref-CFFS2025)] and the smooth quartic three-dimensional argument of Liu et al. (2026, sec. 3 and 5) <a id="pm-cite-55"></a>[[LLWWW2026](#ref-LLWWW2026)]. The latter uses a sum of transition-profile derivatives and a linear joining to the ambient amplitude. A related geometric stability inequality in Chan, Fernández-Real, Figalli, Florit-Simon, et al. (2026, Proposition 4.7) <a id="pm-cite-56"></a>[[CFFFS2026](#ref-CFFFS2026)] uses a weighted ground-state identity on the normal parametrizing manifold. Here we use a square root of a sum of squares and retain its exact gradient variance. The following argument supplies the required estimates on our six-dimensional interface.

The cancellation is already visible for two nonnegative Lipschitz functions $\xi_0,\xi_1$ on a Euclidean open set. Wherever $\xi_0^2+\xi_1^2>0$, differentiation gives $$|\nabla\xi_0|^2+|\nabla\xi_1|^2
 -\left|\nabla\sqrt{\xi_0^2+\xi_1^2}\right|^2
 =\frac{|\xi_0\nabla\xi_1-\xi_1\nabla\xi_0|^2}
        {\xi_0^2+\xi_1^2}.$$ The identity holds almost everywhere with value zero on their common zero set. If $\xi_0=h\cos\theta$ and $\xi_1=h\sin\theta$ for locally Lipschitz $h\ge0$ and $0\le\theta\le\pi/2$, the square root is just $h$ and the variance is $h^2|\nabla\theta|^2$. Thus it cancels the cost of changing amplitudes when the profiles agree. In the construction below, $q$ and the lifted $G$ agree to a controlled error on the short normal segments called accurate. The other short segments are charged to defect mass, while the long segments have exponentially small tails. This is a change from an ambient description to a surface description near bad zeros. The interpolation between neighboring sheet amplitudes in Section <a href="#sec:radial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:radial">1</a> is a separate construction.

<a id="stmt-lem-growth-gluing"></a>
<div id="lem:growth-gluing" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:M3"></a>
**Lemma 40** (Uniform gluing). *Fix $0<\tau\le .001$ among the interface tolerances, before the final choice of $\delta$, and choose the preceding data with this tolerance. There exist $L_0,t_*,c_0>0$ and $R_1,C<\infty$ with the following property. Let $f:[t_{\min},\infty)\to(0,1]$ be nonincreasing and locally Lipschitz, equal to one on $[t_{\min},t_*]$, and a positive multiple of $e^{-\kappa t/2}$ for sufficiently large $t$. Suppose also that $|f'|\le C_f^{(0)}f$ for some finite constant. Then for every center and $R\ge R_1$, writing $s_X=\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(\cdot,X)$, $$\label{eq:M3}
 \begin{aligned}
 &(1-\tau)\int_{\Sigma_g\cap\{s_X\ge4L_0\}}S_0 f(d)^2\chi^2+c_0N_R\\
 &\qquad\le(1+\tau)\int_{\Sigma_g}\chi^2 f'(d)^2|\nabla_\Sigma d|^2
       +\frac C{R^2}\int_{\Sigma_g}\chi^2f(d)^2.
 \end{aligned}$$ In particular $c_0$ is independent of $f$. The constants $L_0,t_*,R_1,C$ can also be chosen independently of $f$; its stated tail and slope conditions are used only to justify exhaustion.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-growth-gluing"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-growth-gluing</summary>

*Proof.* If $X$ is empty, apply <a href="#eq:L3" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L3">[eq:L3]</a> to $\chi f(d)$ and use Young’s inequality. Suppose henceforth that $X$ is nonempty. We construct the test, reserve positive mass for its transition region, and estimate its derivatives. The $f'$ energy and the spatial-weight cost remain on the right side of <a href="#eq:M3" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:M3">[eq:M3]</a>. After estimating those terms and using the mass reserve, only the two partition terms remain to be canceled by the variance identity.

<a id="pm-h-59"></a>
###### Construction: a collar on which $f=1$.

<a id="eq:growth-bounded-collar-reach"></a>
The base tangent-ball estimate in Corollary <a href="#cor:reach-base-defect" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:reach-base-defect">27</a> and <a href="#eq:diffuse-local" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:diffuse-local">[eq:diffuse-local]</a> give a uniform bound $$\label{eq:growth-bounded-collar-reach}
 \sup_{\Sigma_g\cap\{s_X\le10L_0\}}d\le T(\delta,L_0)<\infty.$$ Indeed a nearby $x\in X$ gives $D(z)\ge c\delta e^{-C(10L_0+1)}$, whereas a base tangent ball gives $D(z)\le C_\Gamma/d(z)$ when $d$ is large. The same bound, with a changed constant, applies to the *source* reach $d(z)$ of a plateau pair $(z,y)$ whenever $s_X(y)\le10L_0$, because <a href="#eq:C10" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:C10">[eq:C10]</a> gives $D(y)\le C_\Gamma/d(z)$. Choose $t_*$ to cover both bounds. This choice will also be used in the multiplier argument.

Choose Lipschitz functions on physical space $$\begin{gathered}
 \theta=\theta(s_X/L_0),\qquad
 \theta=0\ (s_X\le2L_0),\qquad
 \theta=\pi/2\ (s_X\ge3L_0),\\
 |\nabla\theta|\le C/L_0,\qquad
 \alpha_0=\cos\theta,\qquad b=\sin\theta.
 \end{gathered}$$ On $\Sigma_g$ choose $\mu=0$ for $s_X\le L_0/2$ and $\mu=1$ for $s_X\ge L_0$, with $|\nabla\mu|\le C/L_0$, and put $p_0(z)=\mu(z)f(d(z))$. Use the normal immersion $\Phi(z,s)=z+s\nu(z)$ and the even curvature cutoff $\beta(z,s)$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:interface-immersed-test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:interface-immersed-test">30</a>, supported strictly inside $|s|a<\alpha$ and equal to one for $|s|a\le\alpha/2$. The parameter $\alpha$ is fixed small in terms of $\tau$ before shrinking $\delta$. The physical ambient function and the lifted functions are $$\xi_0(x)=q(x)\chi(x)\alpha_0(x),\qquad
 \xi_{z,s}(x)=G(s)\beta(z,s)\chi(x)b(x)p_0(z),\quad x=\Phi(z,s).$$ They define the single physical test $F=(\sum_j\xi_j^2)^{1/2}$. Here the sum includes $\xi_0$ and every nonzero lifted preimage of the physical point; no global numbering of the sheets is chosen.

<a id="pm-h-60"></a>
###### Admissibility and the full variance.

<a id="eq:growth-variance"></a>
The immersion need not be globally injective. To justify the physical test without choosing global sheet labels, first cap $|s|$ and physical support by smooth cutoffs. The nonzero lifted support is then compact inside the immersion domain: $|z|$ is bounded because both $|x|$ and $|s|$ are bounded, and $\mu$ keeps the bases away from $X$. Fibers are discrete by local invertibility and finite by compactness. Around any physical point finitely many local inverses therefore contain all nonzero branches. A smaller neighborhood has no additional branch arriving from elsewhere in the compact support. Thus $F$ is locally Lipschitz, including where it vanishes, and is a compactly supported stability test. Almost everywhere, $$\label{eq:growth-variance}
 \sum_j|\nabla\xi_j|^2-|\nabla F|^2
 =\min_{w\in\mathbb R^n}\sum_j|\nabla\xi_j-\xi_jw|^2
 =:\mathcal V\ge0.$$ The potential terms add exactly, even where $3v^2-1$ is negative. Consequently the physical quadratic form is the sum of the ambient and lifted forms *minus* $\int\mathcal V$. No partition of a chart is introduced. The area formula accounts for every lifted preimage. Moreover the variance of any subfamily is a lower bound for $\mathcal V$, since each omitted square is nonnegative before minimization.

For each fixed normal cap remove the spatial cap, and then remove the normal cap. For the lifted terms this is dominated convergence in <a href="#eq:L4" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L4">[eq:L4]</a>, using $$\chi(\Phi(z,s))/\chi(z)\le e^{|s|/R},\qquad
 |\nabla d|\le C\exp\bigl(C\sqrt{\log(K(d)+1)}\bigr),\qquad
 K(d)\le1+Cd.$$ For each fixed $f$ the products $|f'|\,|\nabla d|$ are bounded, and normal cutoff costs are polynomials in $|s|$ times $G(s)^2$. Polynomial good-area growth and the exponential spatial weight dominate the remaining factors. For the subtracted variance retain only the lower bounds on fixed short segments used below, and apply Fatou’s lemma. This proves that the same stability comparison is valid after both caps are removed. The dominating bound here may depend on $f$; it is used only for convergence. No coefficient in the quantitative estimates below uses its value.

<a id="pm-h-61"></a>
###### Claim 1: a positive mass reserve independent of $f$.

The favorable terms will pay for the transition collar while retaining the full stated strength outside it. Define $$S_{\rm sw}=\Sigma_g\cap\{3L_0/2\le s_X\le4L_0\},\qquad
 D_{\rm sw}=\int_{S_{\rm sw}}\chi^2D.$$ Disjoint fixed small balls about the unit-separated bad net yield $c_\delta N_R$ from the term $\int q^3D\chi^2\alpha_0^2$: at their centers $q=1$ and $D\ge\delta$, and unit regularity and <a href="#eq:diffuse-local" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:diffuse-local">[eq:diffuse-local]</a> give uniform lower bounds in the balls. These balls are disjoint from a fixed small normal tube over $S_{\rm sw}$. That tube is injective: small defect gives a single graph in a uniform fixed neighborhood of every base, and any overlapping short normal segments must be in such a chart, where the normal map is injective. In this tube the ambient term gives $$c\int_{S_{\rm sw}}\chi(z)^2\alpha_0(z)^2D(z)
       -\frac C{L_0}D_{\rm sw}-o_{R\to\infty}(1)D_{\rm sw}.$$ Here $q^3D$ is uniformly comparable to $D(z)$, and freezing $\alpha_0$ and $\chi$ accounts for the two errors.

The lifted potential and Jacobian terms of <a href="#eq:L4" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L4">[eq:L4]</a> provide the complementary positive mass with $b(z)^2$, and on $s_X\ge4L_0$ provide $(1-\tau')\sigma\int S_0f^2\chi^2$, where $\tau'>0$ is as small as prescribed. To check the freezing error, on the cutoff tube $$|\partial_sJ|\le C(1+|s|)^nD(z),\qquad J=\det(1+sA),$$ because $|H|\le CD$ and $a^2\le CD$; expand the determinant in powers of $sA$.
