<a id="pm-h-86"></a>
###### The finite tolerance budget.

Since $\sqrt2/\sigma=3/4$, define <a id="eq:radial-loss-number"></a>$$\label{eq:radial-loss-number}
 C(G_1)=\frac34\sup_{u\ge0}
       e^{-\sqrt2u}(1-e^{-1.1G_1u})^2.$$ Set $x=e^{-1.1G_1u}$ and $a_*=\sqrt2/(1.1G_1)$. Differentiation of $x^{a_*}(1-x)^2$ shows its only interior maximum is at $x=a_* /(a_*+2)$, while the endpoint values are zero. Hence <a id="eq:radial-maximum"></a>$$\label{eq:radial-maximum}
 C(G_1)=\frac34
 \left(\frac{a_*}{a_*+2}\right)^{a_*}
 \left(\frac2{a_*+2}\right)^2.$$ The defining expression is nondecreasing in $G_1$, so the largest permitted value occurs at $1.001$, and direct evaluation gives $C(G_1)<.083272621$.

Use Young factors at most $1.001$ for the band derivative, depth cutoff, and cutoff difference. After those fixed choices, increase $R$ until the normal Jacobian, the inverse derivative $\,\mathrm ds_i/\,\mathrm dt$, and the exponential of the additive distance error are each at most $1.001$. After all these finite factors have been fixed, the uniform estimate following <a href="#eq:radial-one-edge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:radial-one-edge">[eq:radial-one-edge]</a> allows a normalized error of at most $.002$ on each side, including the nearest side, *after multiplication by those factors*. This is legitimate because the unamplified uniform error can be made arbitrarily small. The principal normalized switching contribution is consequently at most <a id="eq:radial-finite-budget"></a>$$\label{eq:radial-finite-budget}
 \frac{.083272621}{\tanh(3\sqrt2)}
 \left(1+\frac{.001}{4}\right)^2
 \frac{32}{31.999}(1.001)^6+.004
 <.087853<.100.$$ Every factor is finite: the band width was fixed at $3$, and all analytic tolerances were fixed before $R$ increased. In particular the proof does not use an infinite-width switch and silently discard its tails.

Choose the depth constant $M$ large enough for its negligible cost before the final increase of $R$. Then <a href="#eq:radial-test-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:radial-test-mass">[eq:radial-test-mass]</a> is at least $.998\sigma\mathcal L$, and the sheet-gradient costs in <a href="#eq:radial-tangent-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:radial-tangent-cost">[eq:radial-tangent-cost]</a>, including the fixed Young factor for the depth cutoff, are at most $1.005\sigma\int|\nabla_\Sigma\psi|^2$. The other Young factors multiply only the switch term or a tangential band term tending to zero. The rough collar and outer cutoff errors have already been shown negligible. Substitution into <a href="#eq:q-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:q-stability">[eq:q-stability]</a> and division by $\sigma$ give $$(.998-.100)\mathcal L
 \le1.005\int_\Sigma|\nabla_\Sigma\psi|^2\,\mathrm dS
       +o_R(\mathcal A)+o_{B\to\infty;R}(1).$$ As $.998-.100>.895$, this proves <a href="#eq:R7" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:R7">[eq:R7]</a>. All estimates were made at finite $B$ with coefficients uniform in $B$; the outer limit therefore precedes the limit $R\to\infty$ exactly as required by the radial argument. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-87"></a>
###### The coefficient comparison and the contradiction

The reach and stability inequalities now concern the same sheet amplitude. Removing the derivative of its radial factor exposes the coefficients directly; their strict differences will leave a positive multiple of $\mathcal F T_r/\rho^2$.

<a id="eq:R8"></a>
The cross term against $h$ can be removed exactly: $$\int_\Sigma|\nabla(hm_c\sqrt{W_*})|^2
 =\int_\Sigma\phi|\nabla(m_c\sqrt{W_*})|^2
       -\int_\Sigma h\Delta h\,m_c^2W_* ,
 \qquad
 -\frac{\Delta h}{h}
 =\frac{4+8T_r-2Hz\cdot\nu}{\rho^2}.$$ All terms have compact support in the regular surface. The $H$ term is $o_R(1)\mathcal L$ by <a href="#eq:R1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:R1">[eq:R1]</a> and <a href="#eq:radial-scale-separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:radial-scale-separation">[eq:radial-scale-separation]</a>. The derivatives of $m_c$ have negligible cost by Lemma <a href="#lem:radial-errors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:radial-errors">43</a>; Young’s inequality costs an arbitrarily small fixed relative loss in the other gradient term. Since $$\frac{.73(1.005)}{.895}<.820,\qquad
 \nabla\log W_*=
 \lambda\nabla(c\tau-b(d))-\frac{\vartheta}{d}\nabla d,$$ <a href="#eq:R7" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:R7">[eq:R7]</a>, after absorbing $o_R(1)\mathcal L$, bounds $.73\mathcal L$, up to negligible errors, by $$.835\int_\Sigma\phi m_c^2W_*
 \left[
 \frac{4+8T_r}{\rho^2}
 +\frac{1.03}{4}
 \left|\lambda\nabla(c\tau-b(d))
                 -\frac{\vartheta}{d}\nabla d\right|^2
 \right]\,\mathrm dS .$$ Every comparison with <a href="#eq:R6" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:R6">[eq:R6]</a> is strict. For the gradient square use $|a-b|^2\le1.5|a|^2+3|b|^2$ and $$.835\frac{1.03}{4}\,1.5\lambda^2
 \le .70955\lambda<.72\,\frac{\lambda}{b'}
 \quad(0<\lambda\le2.2,\ 0<b'\le1).$$ Its other term is $.835(1.03)3\vartheta^2/(4d^2)$, smaller than $.012/d$ uniformly on the supported set for large $R$. Where $\lambda=0$ the first comparison is simply unnecessary. The constant radial terms satisfy $.835\cdot4=3.34<3.44$. For the $T_r$ term use $\mathcal F/W_*\ge1/(2.2+o_R(1))$: $$24\frac{\mathcal F}{W_*}-.835\cdot8
 \ge\bigl(24-.835\cdot8(2.2+o_R(1))\bigr)
                 \frac{\mathcal F}{W_*}
 \ge5\frac{\mathcal F}{W_*}.$$ Subtracting and discarding the other nonnegative remainders yields $$\label{eq:R8}
 \int_\Sigma\phi m_c^2\mathcal F\,\frac{T_r}{\rho^2}\,\mathrm dS
 \le o_R(\mathcal A)+o_{B\to\infty;R}(1).$$ Every absorption of $o_R(1)\mathcal L$ above is performed at finite $B$, with its coefficient uniform in $B$. Thus the exhaustion does not presume that $\mathcal L$ has a finite limit as $B\to\infty$.

It remains to find enough surface area where the weight has not decayed. The chosen center supplies energy of order $K(R)R^m$. We first transfer that energy to regular zero-surface area, then use injective normal tubes to exclude a substantial portion at large reach.

<a id="stmt-lem-radial-area"></a>
<div id="lem:radial-area" class="lemma">

**Lemma 47** (Area at small reach). *For all sufficiently large $R$, the set $$\{z\in\Sigma\cap B_{2R}:m_H(z)=1,\ d(z)\le L/2\}$$ has area at least $c K(R)R^m$, with $c>0$ independent of $R$ and of the selected center.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-radial-area"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-radial-area</summary>

*Proof.* The energy in $B_R$ is at least $c K(R)R^m$ by the center choice. We first transfer a fixed fraction of it to good surface area. Let $\mathcal Z$ be a fixed-scale separated net on all of $\Sigma$. Uniform gradient bounds give a fixed positive lower energy in a fixed small ball at every zero, including singular zeros. Packing, the definition of $K$, and ball coverings show that the number of net points in $B_{R+j+2}$ is at most $$C K(R)R^m(1+j/R)^{m+1}.$$ By <a href="#eq:phase-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:phase-decay">[eq:phase-decay]</a> and Modica’s inequality, the energy at points of $B_R$ whose distance from $\Sigma$ is greater than $P_0$ is therefore at most $$C K(R)R^m\sum_{j\ge P_0}
 (1+j/R)^{m+1}(j+2)^n e^{-c_1j}.$$ Choose $P_0$ large and fixed so this is a small fraction of the initial energy. Energy within the enlarged rough collar is also a negligible fraction: its density is bounded and its volume in $B_{2R}$ is $O(K(R)R^{m-4+2\alpha+n\beta_0})$. Enlarge the collar by $2P_0$ without changing this estimate. Every remaining point lies within $P_0$ of a zero $z$ in $B_{2R}$ with $m_H(z)=1$. Such bases have $D\to0$ uniformly. Their fixed normal tubes are regular with bounded Jacobian, and cover the points in question by nearest projection. Their energy is at most a fixed constant times their base area by the area formula and the bounded energy density. Thus $$\mathcal H^m(\Sigma\cap B_{2R}\cap\{m_H=1\})\ge c K(R)R^m.$$

The large-reach part can now be removed with the jointly injective tube estimate from Remark <a href="#rem:reach-ordinary-tube" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rem:reach-ordinary-tube">21</a>, applied with threshold $L/2$. More explicitly, its normal map is injective simultaneously over all bases with $d>L/2$ and on both normal sides for $|t|<c_2L$, where $c_2>0$ depends only on the fixed penalty. Its Jacobian has a fixed positive lower bound. Since $L\le R$, the images of bases in $B_{2R}$ lie in a ball of radius at most $3R$. Consequently $$\mathcal H^m(\Sigma\cap B_{2R}\cap\{d>L/2\})
 \le C R^{m+1}/L
 =C K(R)R^m/\sqrt{K(R)}=o(K(R)R^m).$$ Subtracting this from the preceding good-area lower bound proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

Take $B>3R$ in <a href="#eq:R8" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:R8">[eq:R8]</a>. On the set in Lemma <a href="#lem:radial-area" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:radial-area">47</a>, $m_c=1$, $\rho\le\sqrt5R$ and $T_r\ge1/5$. Since $d\le L/2$ and $f_*(c\tau)=1$, $$\mathcal F(\tau,d)\ge\int_{L/2}^L t^{-\vartheta}\,\mathrm dt
 =\frac{1-2^{-(1-\vartheta)}}{1-\vartheta}L^{1-\vartheta}.$$ Also $\phi T_r/\rho^2\ge cR^{-m}$ on $B_{2R}$. The left side of <a href="#eq:R8" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:R8">[eq:R8]</a> is consequently at least $cK(R)L^{1-\vartheta}=c\mathcal A$. First let $B\to\infty$ at fixed $R$ and then let $R\to\infty$. This contradicts <a href="#eq:R8" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:R8">[eq:R8]</a>. The unbounded-density alternative is impossible.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-radial-area-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-radial-area</summary>

*Completion of Theorem <a href="#thm:stable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:stable">2</a>.* If a solution attains either endpoint of $[-1,1]$, it is the corresponding constant by the strong maximum principle. The remaining constant, zero, is unstable. Indeed, for any nonzero $\chi\in C_c^1(\mathbb R^7)$, the test $\chi_R(x)=\chi(x/R)$ gives $$Q_0(\chi_R)=R^5\int|\nabla\chi|^2-R^7\int\chi^2<0$$ for large $R$. For a nonconstant stable solution with values in $(-1,1)$, if $D$ vanishes anywhere, the equality case in Section <a href="#sec:diffuse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:diffuse">2</a> gives a planar heteroclinic. Otherwise $D>0$ everywhere. If its centered density is bounded, Theorem <a href="#thm:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:finite">3</a> applies. If that density were unbounded, then $K(R)\to\infty$ and Sections <a href="#sec:reach" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reach">1</a>–<a href="#sec:growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:growth">3</a>, followed by the contradiction just proved, would apply. Thus this last case cannot occur, and Theorem <a href="#thm:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:finite">3</a> gives precisely the claimed constants and planar profiles. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-88"></a>
##### The dimension-nine comparison

There are two dimension-sensitive steps in the proof. The classification of the end profiles used Theorem <a href="#thm:stable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:stable">2</a> in dimension seven. The cone argument used the absence of a singular ray in ambient dimension eight. In dimension nine, the singular-set bound allows dimension one, so a vertical cylinder over a singular minimizing cone in $\mathbb R^8$ is no longer excluded. The actual construction of del Pino, Kowalczyk, and Wei realizes precisely this possibility.

<a id="eq:monotone-dpkw-clearing"></a>
<a id="eq:monotone-dpkw-graph"></a>
Here the numbering refers to their published paper (Pino, Kowalczyk, and Wei 2011) <a id="pm-cite-57"></a>[[DPKW2011](#ref-DPKW2011)]. Its Theorem 1, pp. 1488–1489, constructs, for sufficiently small fixed $\alpha>0$, a bounded entire smooth solution $u_\alpha$ in $\mathbb R^9$, strictly increasing in $x_9$, associated with the nonaffine entire minimal graph $$\Gamma_\alpha=\{(p,F_\alpha(p)):p\in\mathbb R^8\},\qquad
 F_\alpha(p)=\alpha^{-1}F(\alpha p).$$ It satisfies $$|u_\alpha(x)|\longrightarrow1
 \quad\text{as }\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,\Gamma_\alpha)\longrightarrow\infty.
 \label{eq:monotone-dpkw-clearing}$$ The graph is a refinement of the Bombieri–De Giorgi–Giusti construction (Bombieri, De Giorgi, and Giusti 1969) <a id="pm-cite-58"></a>[[BDG1969](#ref-BDG1969)]. With $p=(\xi,\zeta)\in\mathbb R^4\times\mathbb R^4$ and $(|\xi|,|\zeta|)=(r\cos\theta,r\sin\theta)$, Lemma 2.1 and Theorem 2 of Pino, Kowalczyk, and Wei (2011, 1492–93) <a id="pm-cite-59"></a>[[DPKW2011](#ref-DPKW2011)] give $$\begin{gathered}
 F_0(r,\theta)=r^3g_{\rm BDG}(\theta),\qquad
 g_{\rm BDG}(\theta)>0\quad(\pi/4<\theta<\pi/2),\\
 F_0\le F\le F_0+C r^{-\beta}\min\{F_0,1\}
       \quad\text{in this sector for }r>R_0,
       \qquad 0<\beta<1,\\
 F(\zeta,\xi)=-F(\xi,\zeta).
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:monotone-dpkw-graph}$$ The angular notation $g_{\rm BDG}$ is unrelated to the heteroclinic $g$. In arXiv version 0806.3141v2 the corresponding main and graph theorems are numbered 1.1 and 3.1, respectively.

We spell out the consequences needed for the comparison. For a fixed base point $p$, continuity bounds $F_\alpha$ on every bounded neighborhood of $p$. Hence $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}((p,t),\Gamma_\alpha)\to\infty$ as $|t|\to\infty$: otherwise a graph point a bounded distance away would have a bounded base coordinate and an unbounded height. Bounded strict monotonicity gives distinct vertical limits, while <a href="#eq:monotone-dpkw-clearing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:monotone-dpkw-clearing">[eq:monotone-dpkw-clearing]</a> puts both limits in $\{-1,1\}$. They are therefore $-1$ and $1$. In particular $-1<u_\alpha<1$ everywhere and each vertical line has a unique zero, of height $h_\alpha(p)$. Equation <a href="#eq:monotone-dpkw-clearing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:monotone-dpkw-clearing">[eq:monotone-dpkw-clearing]</a> also implies that all these zeros lie within a fixed distance $D_\alpha<\infty$ of $\Gamma_\alpha$.

<a id="eq:monotone-dpkw-cubic"></a>
Fix $p$ with $0<|\xi|<|\zeta|$ and fix $t\in\mathbb R$. The angular direction stays in a compact subinterval of the positive sector for all points $Rp+O(1)$ as $R\to\infty$. The lower bound in <a href="#eq:monotone-dpkw-graph" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:monotone-dpkw-graph">[eq:monotone-dpkw-graph]</a>, with $\alpha$ fixed, gives $$F_\alpha(Rp+O(1))\ge c_{p,\alpha}R^3
 \quad\text{for all sufficiently large }R.
 \label{eq:monotone-dpkw-cubic}$$ A graph point within $D_\alpha+1$ of the zero $(Rp,h_\alpha(Rp))$ has base coordinate $Rp+O(1)$, so <a href="#eq:monotone-dpkw-cubic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:monotone-dpkw-cubic">[eq:monotone-dpkw-cubic]</a> implies $h_\alpha(Rp)/R\to+\infty$. Thus $u_\alpha(Rp,Rt)<0$ for large $R$. Moreover the distance from $(Rp,Rt)$ to the graph tends to infinity: a bounded-distance subsequence would contradict <a href="#eq:monotone-dpkw-cubic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:monotone-dpkw-cubic">[eq:monotone-dpkw-cubic]</a>. By <a href="#eq:monotone-dpkw-clearing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:monotone-dpkw-clearing">[eq:monotone-dpkw-clearing]</a>, $$u_\alpha(Rp,Rt)\longrightarrow-1
           \quad\text{if }0<|\xi|<|\zeta|.$$ The oddness of $F$ and the same argument give limit $+1$ in the opposite sector $0<|\zeta|<|\xi|$. The excluded equality set and coordinate axes have Lebesgue measure zero. Bounded convergence now shows local $L^1$ convergence of the rescaled solutions to the two-phase function with interface <a id="eq:monotone-dpkw-cylinder"></a>$$\mathcal C_9=
      \{(\xi,\zeta):|\xi|=|\zeta|\}\times\mathbb R.
 \label{eq:monotone-dpkw-cylinder}$$ This cone has the entire vertical axis as its singular set and is invariant under vertical translations. Its directional normal component is zero on its regular part. Thus it falls into exactly the case that was ruled out by the singular-set bound in Lemma <a href="#lem:monotone-flat-cone" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:monotone-flat-cone">7</a> in dimension eight.

The same solutions are minimizers by Lemma <a href="#lem:monotone-minimizer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:monotone-minimizer">5</a>, applied to their constant end profiles, and consequently have the corresponding $O(R^8)$ energy growth. Thus neither the derived minimizing property nor this energy bound conflicts with the construction. Their nonplanar phase limit also precludes one-dimensionality. The construction extends constantly in additional coordinates to every $N>9$, as explained in Pino, Kowalczyk, and Wei (2011, 1490) <a id="pm-cite-60"></a>[[DPKW2011](#ref-DPKW2011)]: the added second derivatives vanish, and strict monotonicity in $x_9$ is preserved. It supplies no eight-dimensional example.

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The normal-centered circle through two zeros, shown on the side where $ν(z) ⋅ e = s > 0$. Its radius is $ℓ/(2s)$, so the unmodified quotient $ℓ/s$ is twice that radius. The blue arc shows only the local sheet at $z$; the picture asserts neither tangency at $y$ nor that the circle bounds a zero-free ball. The penalized reach replaces $1/s$ by $Γ(s)$ below.",
      "figure_id": "fig:reach-tangent-ball",
      "pdf_page": 35,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:reach-tangent-ball",
          "exact_quote": "The normal-centered circle through two zeros, shown on the\nside where $\\nu(z)\\cdot e=s>0$. Its radius is $\\ell/(2s)$, so the\nunmodified quotient $\\ell/s$ is twice that radius. The blue arc shows\nonly the local sheet at $z$; the picture asse",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1.25cm,y=1.25cm,>=Latex,font=\\small]\n\\draw[gray!65] (0,1.5) circle[radius=1.5];\n\\draw[dashed] (-2.1,0)--(2.1,0) node[right]{$T_z\\Sigma$};\n\\draw[blue!55!black,thick] (-1.15,.13)..controls(-.55,.025)and(-.2,0)..(0,0)\n ..controls(.2,0)and(.55,.025)..(1.15,.13);\n\\draw[->,thick] (0,0)--(0,.85) node[left]{$\\nu(z)$};\n\\draw[->,thick] (0,0)--(1.5,1.5) node[midway,below right]{$\\ell e=y-z$};\n\\draw[dashed,blue!55!black] (0,1.5)--(1.5,1.5)\n node[midway,above]{$\\ell/(2s)$};\n\\fill (0,0) circle(1.6pt) node[below left]{$z$};\n\\fill (1.5,1.5) circle(1.6pt) node[right]{$y\\in\\Sigma$};\n\\fill[blue!55!black] (0,1.5) circle(1.2pt);\n\\node[anchor=east] at(-.08,1.65){center};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A geometric diagram showing a circle centered at a point labeled \"center\" on a vertical normal line through point $z$. A directed arrow labeled $\\nu(z)$ points upward along the normal line from $z$. A horizontal dashed line through $z$ is labeled $T_z\\Sigma$. A solid blue curved arc passes through $z$. A point $y \\in \\Sigma$ lies on the circle. A dashed line segment connects \"center\" to $y \\in \\Sigma$, labeled $\\ell/(2s)$. A straight line segment connects $z$ to $y$, labeled $\\ell e = y - z$."
    },
    {
      "caption": "A schematic local cross-section, not to scale. The colored strips represent fixed-width middle bands. On the part of a band assigned to $z_{i}$ as the larger uncut amplitude, the shorter gap $ℓ_{i} = d(z_{i})/G_{1}$ has vanishing normalized loss. Here $ℓ_{ij}$ is the distance from $z_{i}$ to its first adjacent sheet on the other side. Only that side can require a positive fixed allowance. The proof uses true normal projections and does not assume globally parallel sheets.",
      "figure_id": "fig:radial-interpolation",
      "pdf_page": 91,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:radial-interpolation",
          "exact_quote": "A schematic local cross-section, not to scale. The colored strips represent fixed-width middle bands. On the part of a band assigned to $z_i$ as the larger uncut amplitude, the shorter gap $\\ell_i=d(z_i)/G_1$ has vanishing normalized loss. ",
          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=.75cm,>=Latex,font=\\small]\n\\fill[blue!10](-3,-.95) rectangle (3,-.65);\n\\fill[orange!16](-3,1.1) rectangle (3,1.4);\n\\draw[thick](-3,-1.6).. controls(-1,-1.62)and(1,-1.58)..(3,-1.55);\n\\draw[thick](-3,0)..controls(-1,-.03)and(1,.03)..(3,.06);\n\\draw[thick](-3,2.5)..controls(-1,2.48)and(1,2.53)..(3,2.57);\n\\draw[dashed,->](0,-1.9)--(0,2.9) node[above]{normal line through $z_i$};\n\\fill(0,0)circle(2pt)node[below right]{$z_i$};\n\\draw[<->](-2.4,-1.6)--(-2.4,0)node[midway,left]{$\\ell_i$};\n\\draw[<->](2.4,.06)--(2.4,2.55)node[midway,right]{$\\ell_{ij}$};\n\\node[anchor=west]at(3.5,-.8){nearest band};\n\\node[anchor=west]at(3.5,1.25){possible charged band};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A schematic local cross-section diagram. A vertical line with an arrow at top is labeled \"normal line through $z_i$\". On the line is a point marked $z_i$. Below $z_i$ is a horizontal shaded rectangle labeled \"nearest band\", with a vertical dimension line to the left labeled $\\ell_i$. Above $z_i$ is a horizontal shaded rectangle labeled \"possible charged band\", with a vertical dimension line labeled $\\ell_{ij}$. Horizontal line segments are drawn through or adjacent to $z_i$."
